Security Requirements for a Hardened RustDesk Fork

Every connection method, every session type, and every byte of content — encrypted and mutually password-authenticated from the first byte, by the only path the program offers, and secure by assertion, not merely by default. The bar is SSH.

Normative specification for a hardened fork of upstream RustDesk pinned at 1.4.7 (release commit 0c86d46) with its protocol submodule libs/hbb_common at df6badc. Every architectural claim here was verified by direct read of that exact tree (279 Rust files), cited by file and symbol name. RFC 2119 key words (MUST, MUST NOT, SHALL, SHOULD) are normative.

The thesis, in one paragraph

RustDesk's serverless "Direct IP" mode applies no encryption: the login challenge, the salted password hash, keystrokes, screen, files and clipboard cross the wire as plaintext, so the hash is brute-forceable offline on a GPU farm and an active attacker can inject input into an authenticated session or man-in-the-middle it on first contact — without the password. Upstream declined to fix this. This fork keeps RustDesk's excellent Rust + Flutter engine and replaces its cryptographic judgement: it inserts one mandatory, mutually-authenticated PAKE at the single choke point through which all six inbound transports flow, so that all five session types and every auxiliary content channel are keyed before a single application message is parsed; it makes those properties runtime invariants the program refuses to operate without; and it excises the fetch-and-run updater, the unvalidated native-plugin loader, the remote server-list rewrite, and the trust-anchor overrides that have no place on an internet-exposed host. Decisively, the fork keeps RustDesk's native shape — one binary per platform that is viewer and controlled side, the role chosen at runtime (core_main.rs argv, as upstream) — and makes the exposed box defensible by deletion, not by a separate build: the rendezvous mediator, the relay path, KCP/UDP hole-punch, and LAN discovery are removed from the tree (R-D4, R-SV4, §17), so the box's entire reachable inbound surface is exactly one v4-only, PAKE-gated TCP port — whether it is being viewed, doing the viewing, or both at once (a DMZ jump-host is commonly both: viewed from outside while it views an inner box). Being a viewer adds no inbound surface — an outbound direct-IP connection is a connected socket, not a listener — so the connecting side is held to the same keyed-before-any-message bar as the responder (R-S13). The result is meant to be, on the wire, as defensible as SSH: the PAKE matches SSH on confidentiality, integrity, and first-contact MITM-resistance, and — because it authenticates from a secret password — its first contact is password-authenticated with no trust-on-first-use window, stronger than SSH's unauthenticated first contact, so an unlimited active MITM who does not know the password cannot key, impersonate, relay-inject, or man-in-the-middle the session; it fails the PAKE closed. The password is the sole authenticator: there is no host-key pin and no per-box identity — nothing to store, pin, or compare — and a substitute box that does know the password is deliberately out of scope (password secrecy is assumed, §2). The one residual where SSH still leads is that the app, as a full viewer, decodes attacker-reachable peer media in-process when connected to a hostile peer (Appendix C #2b) — a universal, operationally-bounded residual (connect only to boxes you trust), which the fork SHOULD close by sandboxing the decode path.

1. Purpose, scope & the SSH bar

The fork's objective: reaching a controlled machine by its own address is encrypted and mutually password-authenticated from the first byte, on every connection method and every session type, by the only code path the program offers — and the program asserts these properties and aborts rather than operate without them.

The bar is SSH. Concretely, the fork must match SSH on the channel and authentication properties that matter and may exceed it. One honest calibration up front: because the fork authenticates by password, the apples-to-apples comparison is SSH password authentication — SSH public-key auth carries far more credential entropy, which R-S9's strong-password requirement only partly offsets.

PropertyOpenSSHThis fork (target)
Transport encryptionAEAD after key exchangeXSalsa20-Poly1305 (libsodium secretbox) after the PAKE — same class
Per-message integrity / anti-replayPer-packet MAC + sequencePoly1305 tag + monotonic sequence per frame — same class
Peer authenticationHost key (TOFU / known_hosts) plus a separate user password/key stepOne mutual PAKE (both sides prove the password in a single exchange) — a network attacker without the password cannot key, impersonate, relay, or MITM — matches SSH-password-auth on the channel, and its first contact is password-authenticated with no trust-on-first-use window, so stronger than SSH's unauthenticated first contact. No host-key pin: peer identity rests on the shared password alone — a substitute that does not know it fails closed; one that does is out of scope (§2)
Offline-crackable material on the wireNone (auth inside the encrypted channel)None on the wire (PAKE transcript reveals nothing brute-forceable) — same; at rest: comparable — the controlled side holds a memory-hard salted hash (Argon2id) as the PRS (R-S9), like SSH's salted hash; the only residual gap is the balanced-PAKE caveat — that stored hash is connect-equivalent (it can key a session), whereas SSH's hash is a pure verifier
Plaintext / downgrade fallbackNone in modern configsNone — the plaintext path is deleted, not defaulted off
Brute-force resistanceRate-limit / fail2ban (online only)One online guess per attempt (PAKE) + the per-IP limiter re-keyed onto the PAKE (R-S10) — ≥ SSH
Implementation maturity (channel crypto)Decades of auditSSH still leads on maturity — the in-tree CPace implementation has had one independent expert review (2026-07-02, findings resolved — §11, docs/CRYPTO-AUDIT-2026-07-02.md) but none of SSH's decades of third-party scrutiny
Attacker-reachable content parsingShell / byte stream only — no media codecs in the clientAs a viewer, the app decodes peer video/audio/clipboard through in-process C codecs (libvpx/libopus/zstd plus media-adjacent native parsers) when connected to a hostile peer — SSH leads here (a universal documented residual, Appendix C #2b — bounded operationally, connect only to peers you trust, and closable by sandboxing the decode path). AV1/libaom is runtime-quarantined and removed as a dependency rather than accepted as a viewer codec (Appendix C #87/#88). In its controlled role the box never decodes peer video (it encodes its own screen); its only inbound decode is then post-PAKE from the already-authenticated peer — parity for that role

Operator's note on man-in-the-middle. The operator considers active MITM a lesser concern in practice (a wrong desktop is noticed quickly; the password gate already raises the bar). The fork nonetheless closes MITM by construction via the mutual PAKE — so this requirement is met "for free" and is not relaxed; it simply is not the load-bearing reason for the design.

Three scoping decisions are load-bearing and non-negotiable:

  • All connection methods and all session types. Six inbound transports, five session kinds, and every auxiliary content channel (§4) — not just screen control.
  • Secure by assertion. Every property below is backed by a runtime, build-time, or test-time check that fails closed (§9).
  • Both roles, one binary. Upstream ships one cross-platform binary that is viewer and controlled side, selected at runtime by argv (core_main.rs); the fork keeps exactly that — every box runs the same package and may be viewer, controlled host, or both at once (a DMZ jump-host is both). Both roles are in scope. The exposed box's reachable mode set is reduced to one not by compiling a role out, but by deleting the rendezvous/relay/UDP-punch/LAN machinery (R-D4, R-SV4, §17) so its only inbound surface is the single keyed TCP port, and by making the hardened policy unconditional — no un-hardened build exists. All shipped platforms are in scope (§3, §12, §16).

Licensed, like upstream, under AGPLv3; corresponding source for every published binary MUST be published.

2. Threat model

Adversary: an active attacker on the network path (café/transit Wi-Fi, ISP, any transited AS, or a host already on the LAN) who can read, drop, modify, replay and inject packets and can initiate connections to the controlled machine's listening port; and, separately, a malicious or compromised rendezvous server the operator might otherwise rely on. The adversary does not know the password. The controlled machine is reachable from the internet (home-router DMZ on a static address). We defend the channel and the host's exposed surface; we do not defend a compromised endpoint or an adversary who knows the password of the box being connected to. (Endpoint substitution — a different box answering at the same address via an IP/route/DNS/DHCP/LAN hijack — splits cleanly by whether the substitute knows the password: a non-password-knowing substitute is a network adversary and is in scope — CPace fails it closed exactly as it fails any MITM, since it cannot key without the password; a password-knowing substitute (reachable only by password reuse across boxes, a leak, or prior compromise) is out of scope, the same scope-out as the password-knower above, because password secrecy is assumed. The fork carries no host-key pin — the password is the sole authenticator, and this is intended: a balanced PAKE over a secret password is precisely the primitive that defeats unlimited active MITM, and the retired host-key pin defended only the out-of-scope password-knowing substitute.) Both roles are in scope (§1), in one binary: the app also initiates connections (any box can be a viewer — including the internet-exposed DMZ box reaching an inner box it controls), so the adversary additionally includes a malicious peer the operator is induced to connect to — a poisoned or mistyped address, a QR code, or a rustdesk:// deep link — against which the app holds the initiator to the same keyed-before-any-message bar as the responder (R-S13), and treats every byte a connected peer returns as attacker-controlled (§4.4). Initiating adds no inbound attack surface: an outbound direct-IP connection is a connected socket (fixed peer 4-tuple), not a listener. So even on the exposed box, acting as a viewer opens no new port — its reachable inbound surface stays the single keyed TCP port, because the rendezvous/UDP-punch/LAN machinery that would otherwise bind wildcard (0.0.0.0) sockets is deleted (R-D4, R-SV4, §17), not merely unused.

Failure mode A — offline-crackable credentials

Upstream sends salt+challenge in cleartext and the answer is a fast, unstretched SHA-256(SHA-256(pw‖salt)‖challenge); captured once, brute-forced offline on a GPU farm. Online limits are irrelevant. No credential material may be recoverable from the transcript.

Failure mode B — passwordless MITM & injection

No channel encryption ⇒ no message authentication. Upstream checks the password once, flips self.authorized=true (connection.rs), then trusts every subsequent frame; an active attacker injects input into the authenticated stream, or MITMs first contact, without the password. Authorization must be a property of a mutually-authenticated, encrypted channel.

Failure mode C — host expansion (pivot, RCE, re-homing)

An exposed controlled host must not be a launch point. This fork additionally treats the updater, the plugin loader, the server-pushed config rewrite, the rustdesk:// deep-link writes, and the os_login → PAM desktop-session-start as parts of the attack surface to be excised (§8) — these are reachable by the network or a malicious rendezvous server without the password, so they are squarely in scope. (The file system, reachable only by an already-authenticated peer, is deliberately a single unconfined, full-filesystem mode per R-S8 and §2 — confining it would mean defending against a password-knower, which we do not, and a confinement toggle is itself forbidden by R-S12.)

3. What is being forked — composition & version pinning

"RustDesk" is not one repository and the controlled side is not a separate program. Pinned from the cloned tree:

ComponentKindFork?Role
rustdesk/rustdesk @ 1.4.7 (0c86d46)Main repo (Rust core + Flutter UI in-tree under flutter/)YesUpstream: one cross-platform binary = viewer and controlled side (--server), role by argv (core_main.rs). The fork keeps this one-binary-both-roles model — R-R2/R-R2a, §16 — and hardens it unconditionally (the PAKE choke point, the direct-only deletions of the rendezvous/relay/UDP machinery, the pinned policy), with the role chosen at runtime exactly as upstream.
rustdesk/hbb_common @ df6badcThe only git submodule (libs/hbb_common)YesProtobuf protocol, transport, and the secretbox session-crypto layer (src/tcp.rs). The PAKE spans both repos.
libs/{scrap,enigo,clipboard,portable}In-tree workspace members (not submodules)InheritedCapture/codecs, input, clipboard/CLIPRDR, packer.
rustdesk/rustdesk-server (hbbs/hbbr)Separate repoNoRendezvous/relay servers. Serverless by design (§14).

3.1 Version pinning — the build is fully pinnable

The repository ships a committed Cargo.lock with 910 locked crates, of which 38 are git-sourced and every one is pinned to an exact commit SHA in the lockfile. Version-pinning the build therefore means commit and build from the lockfile, not chase moving branches.

R-B5aMUST Build only from the committed Cargo.lock; pin the toolchain
CI MUST build with --locked. The toolchain MUST be pinned to the upstream-verified versions: Rust 1.75 (macOS 1.81; the 32-bit Sciter path's old nightly is removed with Sciter, below), Flutter 3.24.5, Android NDK r28c, vcpkg @ commit 120deac3 (with the custom overlay ports in res/vcpkg), LLVM 15.0.6. The vcpkg native deps (libvpx, libyuv, opus, libjpeg-turbo, oboe, cpu-features) build from that pinned baseline. Crypto is the sodiumoxide crate (libsodium), not vcpkg.
R-B5bSHOULD Re-pin git deps to your own mirrors over time
28 of the 38 git-sourced lockfile records are rustdesk-org/* forks of common crates (arboard, rdev, cpal, magnum-opus, hwcodec, wezterm/pty, and related desktop/audio/input crates). They are rev-locked — reproducible, and have now been diffed against upstream at these exact SHAs and found benign or security-positive where security-relevant — but a fork still inherits supply-chain trust in that org. Mirror the security-relevant ones, preserving the rustdesk-org hardening commits — not re-pinning to pristine crates.io releases, which would regress safety. The pin/mirror MUST also cover non-rustdesk-org git code with runtime or build reachability, including bjornsnoen/x11-rs, clslaid/x11-clipboard, clslaid/cacao, tauri-apps/tray-icon, and the macOS Foundation/Cidre binding forks. No crate's build.rs may fetch from the network (cpal/asio-sys pulls the ASIO SDK from steinberg.net — gated off on Linux; vendor any such SDK). The same applies to the Dart rustdesk-org forks (flutter/pubspec.lock has 7 rustdesk-org Dart forks; 8 git pins total — the eighth is window_size from google/flutter-desktop-embedding, Appendix D) — especially uni_links, the pre-authentication deep-link receiver.

3.2 The canonical pin table — every version the build fixes (observed in 1.4.7)

One source of truth, so the implementation agent never resolves "latest." Every value is read from the pinned tree (0c86d46 / hbb_common df6badc) at the cited location; the fork MUST reproduce them exactly (R-B5a, R-B7) and add the one pin upstream omits — a committed rust-toolchain.toml (R-R1).

cpu-features and oboe are Android-only. The native set — libvpx, libyuv, opus, libjpeg-turbo — builds host + target. vram is Windows-only; unix-file-copy-paste is the non-Windows clipboard-file feature. The fork drops hwcodec/vram, ffmpeg's HW paths (amf/nvcodec/qsv), and AV1/libaom on every target — software vpx only for video (R-R2b, Appendix C #88).

4. The inherited attack surface (verified at 1.4.7)

This section is descriptive ground truth — the requirements in §7–§9 act on it. Everything here was read directly from the cloned tree.

4.1 Inbound transports — six, all funnel through one choke point

There is no always-on direct listener by default; the controlled side dials out to a rendezvous server and opens per-session inbound sockets when it receives signaling (PunchHole/FetchLocalAddr/RequestRelay). The exception is the optional direct_server. The secure column is the boolean upstream passes to create_tcp_connection; false ⇒ the secretbox is never keyed ⇒ plaintext.

#TransportAccept (file · symbol)PortEncrypted nowDisposition
1Direct TCP (direct_server) — the serverless pathrendezvous_mediator.rs → create_tcp_connection(secure=false)21118 (if direct-server=Y)PLAINTEXTPAKE-key (mandatory; the whole point)
2Punch-hole TCPserver.rs → create_tcp_connection(true)ephemeralSignedId/PublicKeyPAKE-key
3Intranet / LAN-direct TCPrendezvous_mediator.rs → accept_connection(true)ephemeralyesPAKE-key
4Relay TCPserver.rs create_tcp_connection(secure=rr.secure)21117attacker-controlled (the RequestRelay.secure protobuf field, rendezvous_mediator.rs)PAKE-key, unconditionally (ignore rr.secure)
5KCP over UDP (punch-UDP)kcp_stream.rs → create_tcp_connection(true) (via rendezvous_mediator.rs)ephemeral UDPKCP plaintext + wrapperPAKE-key (+ pre-PAKE kcp-sys parser, §4.3)
6IPv6 UDP/KCPrendezvous_mediator.rs → KCP → create_tcp_connection(true)ephemeral UDP6same as 5PAKE-key
—LAN discovery (UDP broadcast)lan.rs bind(0.0.0.0:21119)21119N/A (ping→pong)discloses MAC/ID/host/username/platform — block/sanitize
—WebSocket / WebRTC inboundnone — from_tcp_stream has zero callers; webrtc feature off——dead code — confirm fork never enables
—Local IPC (UI⇄service)ipc.rs Unix socket / named pipefilesystemlocal-onlytransport already SO_PEERCRED/per-uid-gated upstream — add main-channel write allowlist (R-S11)

The single choke point: all six session transports construct a Stream and hand it to create_tcp_connection (src/server.rs), which today calls stream.set_key() for the secure case before Connection::start. Default serverless exposure is one port (21118), and only if direct-server=Y. Every UDP source in this table (rows 5–6 and LAN discovery) ignites solely from RendezvousMediator::start_all (verified — server.rs), so excising the mediator (R-D4) removes them together; and because every session byte — remote control, files, clipboard, view-camera, terminal, and audio/voice (verified: audio_service.rs sends AudioFrame protobufs on conn.stream, never a UDP/RTP side-channel) — rides this one keyed TCP stream (§4.3), zero UDP costs no functionality. The hardened box's reachable surface is therefore exactly this single v4-only port (R-D3, R-D5) — understood and accountable, not merely firewalled.

4.2 The pre-authentication message surface

"Pre-auth" = processed while self.authorized==false. The gate is the single } else if self.authorized { at connection.rs; only five message arms execute before it (one benign), plus per-connection setup in on_open:

  • on_open: emits Hash{salt,challenge} unconditionally (a pre-auth oracle, by design).
  • Misc/CloseReason: deliberately placed ahead of the gate so a peer can close pre-auth — benign: logs the reason and tears down the session, parses no other attacker data, grants nothing.
  • LoginRequest: the highest-value pre-auth surface — stores the full request, parses OptionMessage, performs a trusted-device 2FA bypass keyed on attacker-supplied hwid/id, and on some branches starts the desktop/CM session — notably a PAM-authenticated X-session start driven by the attacker's os_login credentials (linux_desktop_manager.rs; a second, root-context credential path excised by R-X14) — and sends a Telegram 2FA push before the password is checked.
  • Auth2fa: TOTP check; persists a trusted device from attacker fields on success.
  • TestDelay: a free pre-auth echo/reflector.
  • SwitchSidesResponse: an authorization path that bypasses the password on a matching 10-second UUID, and sets from_switch which also skips 2FA.

Direct-path note: the SignedId/PublicKey key-bootstrap parse is gated if secure (server.rs) and the direct listener passes secure=false, so on the direct path that parse — and AddrMangle::decode — do not run pre-auth (both are mediator/KCP/relay-only); the only attacker-byte parsers before keying are the frame codec and the CPace protobuf (R-P14). Separately, lan.rs parses unauthenticated UDP-broadcast datagrams and replies with host/user info — a distinct pre-auth parser + info-leak, excised by R-X5.

Pre-auth DoS — unbounded frame length

The frame codec's max_packet_length defaults to usize::MAX and set_max_packet_length is called nowhere in the tree (verified). The length header allows ~1 GiB frames; buffering grows with the declared length. Any peer that reaches a listener can force ~1 GiB/connection memory amplification before authentication.

4.3 What rides the keyed stream — and the two escapes

Verified: every byte of peer-bound content, for all five session types, rides the single keyed conn.stream as a protobuf Message — remote control, file transfer, view-camera, terminal, clipboard text and CLIPRDR file bytes, whiteboard input, and audio/voice. Auxiliary "IPC" channels (CM, audio, whiteboard, portable-service shared memory) are localhost privilege-separation only and never touch the network. So one PAKE at the choke point covers all of it — with exactly two exceptions:

  1. Port-forward / RDP tunnel payload. After login, both ends call set_raw() (connection.rs server; port_forward.rs client), which drops the secretbox (tcp.rs) — so the tunnelled RDP/VNC/SSH bytes cross the link in plaintext, even though the auth was keyed.
  2. File-transfer path confinement. File names are well-validated (fs.rs rejects .., null, absolute, UNC, and symlink components), but the destination base directory is attacker-supplied and unconfined — an authenticated peer may write/overwrite/rename/recursively-delete anywhere the service user can reach (~/.ssh, /etc, C:\Windows). There is also an acknowledged symlink TOCTOU (path-validated, then opened by path; the openat/O_NOFOLLOW fix is deferred upstream).

4.4 The initiator's (viewer's) outbound surface

The same binary is also the viewer, and §1 puts both roles in scope — so the connecting side is audited symmetrically, not assumed safe. These surfaces exist wherever the app runs — including the internet-exposed box when it acts as a viewer (a DMZ jump-host reaching an inner box it controls). The spec keys each of them (R-S13); crucially, none is an inbound listener — initiating is an outbound connected socket, not a port — so they add nothing to the exposed box's reachable surface, and the one piece that would open a wildcard (0.0.0.0) socket (the KCP/UDP-punch path, #3 below) is deleted outright (R-D4 / R-S13(d)), not merely keyed. Verified in the cloned tree, the initiator carries four surfaces the responder analysis (§4.1–4.3) does not cover, and the spec's keying requirements (§7) act on each:

  1. An unkeyed direct-IP path — the initiator twin of failure modes A & B. When the viewer dials a bare address, Client::_start returns a live stream with no keying for both the IP case (client.rs, is_ip_str) and the domain:port case — both return before secure_connection is ever reached, and the only client-side is_secured() read (io_loop.rs) merely sets a UI lock-icon, never aborts. So the flagship serverless mode (§17: a client reaches <static-IPv4>:21118) runs plaintext on the connecting side: the login hash is captured for offline cracking and first contact is MITM-able, exactly as on the controlled side. Keying the responder (R-S1) alone does not close this.
  2. A wide pre-key decoder. On the rendezvous/relay path the viewer calls Message::parse_from_bytes over the full application union before keying (client.rs, ahead of conn.set_key), on frames whose length is unbounded (set_max_packet_length is never called on the client path) — the initiator-side twin of the responder's wide-decoder + ~1 GiB DoS surface (§4.2).
  3. The kcp-sys C parser, linked for the viewer. mod kcp_stream is ungated (lib.rs) and the viewer reaches the C reassembly parser pre-key via KcpStream::connect (client.rs, from udp_nat_connect) — so excising the mediator (R-D4) does not by itself remove KCP from the connecting role.
  4. The deep-link auto-connect. rustdesk://<id>?password=…&/r@<server>?key=… initiated an outbound session with no confirmation, honoring an embedded password, relay, and — through the Rust initialize (client.rs, written into other_server) — an embedded trust-anchor key, delivered over unauthenticated local transports (Linux D-Bus, Windows window messages, macOS URL IPC, Android intent). The fork's deep-link surface is connect-only: credential/config/trust-anchor writes are ignored, embedded secrets and relay syntax are stripped/rejected, connect is confirmation-gated, and desktop existing-instance forwarding is either self-handled or delivered through authenticated URL IPC rather than public window messages or D-Bus.

Beyond keying, a viewer induced to connect to a hostile peer feeds attacker-controlled bytes into the in-process native decoders — libvpx/libyuv (VP8/VP9 video), libopus (audio), zstd (cursor/clipboard/file blocks), and the Windows CLIPRDR C parser (the io_loop.rs dispatch). AV1/libaom is runtime-quarantined and no longer linked (Appendix C #87/#88). The PAKE authenticates the peer but cannot make a password-correct-but-hostile box safe; this residual is bounded operationally (deliberate-connect + channel restriction, R-S13/R-X6), not cryptographically, and is recorded as such (Appendix C #2b). Substitution splits by the password: a substitute that does not know the password fails the PAKE before any decode (no key, no Message loop), so this surface is reachable only by a peer whose PAKE succeeded — one the operator deliberately connected to with the right password; a password-knowing substitute could reach it but is out of scope (§2). This residual is universal — every box runs the full viewer, so any box decodes a connected peer's media; there is no build that compiles the remaining decoders out. It is bounded operationally (connect only to peers you trust, plus the R-S13/R-X6 channel restriction), recorded as a documented residual (Appendix C #2b), and the fork SHOULD close it by sandboxing the decode path. When a box acts purely in its controlled role (being viewed, not viewing) it decodes no peer video at all — its only inbound decode is then post-PAKE opus/zstd from the already-authenticated peer (§13), the narrow surface §2 already accepts.

5. Definitions

PAKE. A protocol by which two parties sharing only a password derive a strong, mutually-authenticated session key with no certificates or pre-shared keys; a network attacker without the password cannot complete it, cannot MITM it, and learns nothing offline-crackable — reduced to one online guess per attempt. Balanced = symmetric (both hold the same password) = the "me and my own machine" case.

Mutual key confirmation. After deriving the shared key, each side sends a MAC (keyed by that shared key, over its own handshake message) and verifies the peer's — proving both derived the same key, i.e. both knew the password. One-sided proof is not authentication; it is exactly the hole that sank the prior community fork (a constant b"direct-ok" reply). Exact construction: R-P3.

Transcript binding. Tying the derived keys to everything negotiated — both ephemeral public values, the session id sid, and the protocol/version label carried in the channel identifier CI — so no part can be spliced, downgraded, or replayed cross-protocol. (There are no long-term identities to bind; see R-P5.)

The five session types. The protobuf LoginRequest.oneof union (and the server enum AuthConnType{Remote,FileTransfer,PortForward,ViewCamera,Terminal}): remote control, file transfer, port-forwarding (incl. tunnelled RDP as PortForward{host:"RDP",port:0}), view-camera, remote terminal. The kind is read after login — which is why the stream must be keyed before it.

Excise. Remove from the build so the path cannot be reached, configured back on, or accidentally re-enabled — stronger than "disabled by default."

6. Functional requirements (preserve)

R-F1MUST Preserve all five session types (all fully available; terminal pinned ON by the pinned policy — R-X8)
Remote control, file transfer, port-forward (incl. RDP), view-camera, terminal — all preserved as code, all fully available, and all secured (§7). Note (terminal): post-PAKE the terminal spawns a shell as the service user — root on the exposed box (R-D3) — with no Linux re-auth (should_use_terminal_os_login_scope is Windows-only, connection.rs; the Linux token path is a stub), so one PAKE password yields a direct root shell. On this sovereign box that is not an over-grant to defend against: the CPace-authenticated peer is the trusted owner who already holds the box's shared OS/sudo password (§2 explicitly does not confine a password-knower — "confining it would mean defending against a password-knower, which we do not"), so they reach root from the desktop anyway (xterm → sudo); the fork's yardstick is SSH, which grants a full shell after one auth, so denying the terminal would make this box strictly weaker than the bar. The terminal is therefore pinned ON by the immutable enable-terminal = Y policy pin (R-S16/R-X8), returned by the Config::get_option funnel and — still — rejected by is_option_can_save for every runtime write, so the Permission::terminal gate resolves true as the box's single, un-flippable mode. What stays excised is the genuinely separate second OS credential: the Windows LogonUserW terminal token and the os_login→PAM desktop-start (R-X14/R-S18), so an attended terminal runs as the service user, never via a peer-supplied OS credential — granting the terminal re-introduces no PAM/LogonUserW step. The session-type code stays in the one binary — "all five types" holds and all are reachable; a different policy is a build-time choice (R-F4), never a runtime knob.
R-F2MUST Preserve GUI file transfer, session persistence, parallel & multi-machine connections
Drag-and-drop transfer; reconnect to the same live desktop; phone + laptop at once; more than one controlled machine.
R-F3MUST Preserve "connect by address, from anywhere, any device"
No server, account, VPN, or pre-shared key may become required to connect.
R-F4MUST The direct port is a single pinned compile-time constant (21118)
The direct listener binds one TCP port, pinned at compile time to the literal 21118 (a const DIRECT_PORT: i32 = 21118 — not the inherited RENDEZVOUS_PORT + 2 derivation at rendezvous_mediator.rs, which would silently shift the port and desync the §10.4 CI KAT be16(21118)=527e if RENDEZVOUS_PORT ever changed) — not a runtime option, env var, or config key. It folds into the PAKE channel binding CI (R-P1), so it is a one-mode constant, not a knob; an operator who needs a different port changes the constant and rebuilds (a build-time choice, never a runtime mode). So the host's "one audited port" discipline holds, and no dynamic UDP range is needed for the serverless path.

7. Security requirements (the core)

R-S1MUST An unconditional mutual PAKE at the single choke point is the only way to connect
The PAKE (§10) MUST run inside create_tcp_connection (server.rs) before Connection::start, keying the stream for all six transports of §4.1. The secure parameter MUST be deleted (not honored) — note the direct path passes secure=false, so a naïve "encrypt if secure" would skip the very path we care about; and the relay path's secure is attacker-controlled (rr.secure) and MUST be ignored. The same bar binds the initiator (R-S13): the viewer's unkeyed direct-IP and domain:port early-returns (client.rs, §4.4) MUST be deleted and every viewer initiation routed through the CPace initiator handshake (R-P14) before any Message is parsed — keying the responder alone leaves the connecting side plaintext. When a box runs purely as the exposed --server it never initiates, so the connecting side is unreachable there; if it also views (a jump-host, §17), R-S13's keyed-initiator bar applies and being a viewer adds no inbound surface (an outbound connect is not a listener). The plaintext path, the unverified-trust escape hatch, and the manual fingerprint ceremony MUST be deleted.
R-S2MUST Authorization collapses into the PAKE — one keyed fact, not a re-gated legacy gate
Since the CPace PRS is the permanent password (R-P1, R-S9), a successful mutual key-confirmation (R-P3) already proves both sides know the password before Connection::start. The fork therefore collapses the inherited authorization state machine into the CPace one rather than re-gating it: self.authorized=true (connection.rs) MUST be set only on the CPace KEYED success edge (R-P14a), and the inherited post-keying second authentication — the Hash{salt,challenge} emission (on_open), the validate_password/verify_h1 challenge-response, the 30-s is_recent_session re-admit, the SwitchSidesResponse UUID password-bypass with its 2FA-skip, and the CM operator-accept edge ipc::Data::Authorize → send_logon_response_and_keep_alive (arm, call, a grant with no LoginRequest password check) — MUST be deleted or, where retained, sound only because it is downstream of the PAKE — and on the exposed box's headless --service there is no CM window, so the operator-accept arm is unreachable — never re-gated. Re-gating would leave several redundant grant paths into, each a separate gate to implement correctly on a 6,000-line file whose on_message chain is pre-auth by default; the collapse leaves one. The challenge-response proves the same secret CPace already proved — two locks keyed alike are one lock — so deleting it loses no assurance (R-S6). What survives: LoginRequest is kept only for its non-credential payload (the session-type oneof → AuthConnType, OptionMessage, version); its password/os_login/hwid fields are dropped (R-X7/R-X14). TOTP is excised, not kept: the second factor (Auth2fa) and its GUI enrollment ceremony (generate2fa/verify2fa, auth_2fa.rs) are excised entirely (R-X7), and R-D6 pins the 2fa option empty — so require_2fa is permanently None and the entire Auth2fa + trusted-device subsystem is un-instantiable dead surface (the OTP/terminal pattern, §8). Keeping it would also re-admit a KEYED-but-not-yet-authorized sub-state — a second-factor gate sits between keying and authorization — contradicting this requirement's own single edge into authorized (R-A2). So TOTP, require_2fa, the Auth2fa arm, the trusted-device store, and the totp-rs dependency are excised with the rest (R-X7) — leaving authorized == KEYED literally true. A standalone second factor is out of scope for this single-owner box; CPace + the strong permanent password (R-S9) is the bar.
R-S3MUST Every post-handshake byte is encrypted + integrity-protected + anti-replay
The PAKE-derived per-direction keys (R-P2) MUST drive the secretbox layer for the whole connection; each frame carries a Poly1305 tag and a monotonic sequence; any auth/sequence failure aborts (fail-closed).
R-S4MUST All five types + all auxiliary content channels are covered
Keying the single stream before the oneof type is parsed covers remote/file/port-forward/camera/terminal and the clipboard-text, CLIPRDR file bytes, whiteboard input, and audio channels (all verified to ride conn.stream). The fork MUST NOT implement the handshake per-feature.
R-S5MUST Seal the port-forward / RDP set_raw() escape
The key-strip at connection.rs and port_forward.rs MUST be removed so tunnelled bytes stay inside the secretbox — or the forward MUST be refused. There is no opt-in plaintext mode — a default-off-but-reachable raw tunnel is exactly what the §1 bar ("the plaintext path is deleted, not defaulted off"), R-S12, and acceptance criterion 3 ("the set_raw escape is sealed") forbid. Initiator note: removing the client-side strip (port_forward.rs) is necessary but not sufficient — the viewer MUST also assert the stream it tunnels was PAKE-keyed (is_secured(), R-S13), since the inherited direct-IP initiator path produced an unkeyed stream in the first place (§4.4); a sealed strip over an unkeyed stream still tunnels plaintext. (When a box runs purely as --server it never initiates, so this note is moot there; the server-side seal above always applies.) This one in-band plaintext escape is closed, not toggled.
R-S6MUST No offline-crackable credential material on the wire
The balanced PAKE provides this structurally — the transcript reveals nothing brute-forceable and the attacker is reduced to one online guess per interaction, bounded further by the per-IP rate limiter (R-S10 — which the fork MUST re-key onto the PAKE outcome, since the existing limiter sits on the now-removed password path) and the required strong password (R-S9). The upstream fast SHA-256 challenge/response (the Hash{salt,challenge} emission and validate_password/verify_h1) MUST be deleted outright — R-S2 collapses authorization into the PAKE, so this redundant second proof of the same secret is removed, not merely demoted from the security boundary (keeping it would leave dead pre-auth code and a live pre-auth Hash oracle). The at-rest PRS is itself a memory-hard salted hash — by a different rationale than augmented-PAKE verifier-hardening: a balanced PAKE has no stored verifier for an Argon2/scrypt to protect (that is Argon2's only job in an augmented scheme), so the fork's Argon2id is not a wire-secrecy measure (the transcript is already structurally clean, above) but an at-rest one — it makes the stored PRS a memory-hard salted hash rather than the plaintext password (R-P1/R-S9); the salt is a fixed domain-separation constant (R-P1), so both serverless ends derive an identical PRS from the password alone with no exchange. Closes failure mode A.
R-S7MUST Cap frame length before authentication
The fork MUST call set_max_packet_length to a small bound — ≤ 4 KiB pre-PAKE (only the ≤120-B CPace messages of R-P14 flow before keying) — on every inbound connection before the PAKE completes, and a sane ceiling after — ≈ 32 MiB (far below the variable-length header's 1 GiB ceiling, well above the 128 KiB file block at fs.rs and any realistic video keyframe; the inherited 256 KiB speculative-prealloc cap at bytes_codec.rs bounds only the initial reserve, not the declared length), closing the ~1 GiB/connection pre-auth memory-amplification DoS (§4.2). Pre-PAKE protobuf is the only attacker-reachable parser before keying — and it MUST decode only the dedicated Cpace message (R-P14), via parse_from_bytes::<Cpace>, never the full application Message union — so the reachable decoder is the handshake's fixed fields, not the whole schema (minimize what is decoded, not merely what is acted on). The kcp-sys KCP packet parser is dropped: KCP is driven solely by the rendezvous mediator (rendezvous_mediator.rs), which R-D4 compiles out — and the fork gates mod kcp_stream out entirely (no KCP in either role, since both connect over TCP), so that C parser is absent for both roles, not merely minimized. This cap is the load-bearing pre-auth DoS control: because set_max_packet_length is currently never called anywhere in the tree, the ~1 GiB/connection amplification is the dominant pre-auth memory-DoS — a larger exposure than the per-IP limiter (R-S10) addresses, and it fires per-connection regardless of source IP, so the frame cap is primary and the limiter only a secondary flood-shed. Post-key inflation twin: the receive-path decompress is zstd::decode_all with no output bound (hbb_common/src/compress.rs) — the frame cap bounds the input block but not the decompressed size, so a small compressed file-block, clipboard, or cursor payload from a keyed peer amplifies to an unbounded allocation/disk-write on either role. The fork MUST cap decompressed output (a streaming bound or set_max_output_size) on every receive decompress — the post-key twin of this pre-auth frame cap.
R-S8MUST Close the symlink TOCTOU; file transfer is a single full-filesystem mode (no confinement toggle)
File names are already well-validated (fs.rs rejects .., null, absolute, UNC, symlink components); only the destination base is attacker-supplied. Per §2 the authenticated peer holds the password and is the trusted owner — and remote control already drives the box at the service privilege — so file transfer legitimately operates at that privilege (root on the exposed box) with full filesystem reach, as a single mode; this capability MUST be documented. The fork MUST NOT add a confinement option (a configurable allowed-root): a security-relevant toggle is exactly what R-S12 forbids — it is state that can be misconfigured, it would regress the legitimate "put a file anywhere on my own machine" use, and confining file transfer while the root desktop stays reachable is pointless (the peer has root either way). What is not optional is write-path safety: every write MUST use handle/descriptor-based no-follow opens (openat + O_NOFOLLOW / Windows equivalent) to close the acknowledged symlink TOCTOU (§4.3) — defending the peer's own writes against a local symlink swap, a correctness guarantee asserted on every write (R-A5), not a confinement feature. Consistency with R-X8: the discipline R-X8 applies to the terminal is toggle-and-surface removal (removing the default-on flippable toggle and its redundant surface to pin the capability ON), not capability denial — so keeping file transfer as one full-FS mode while adding no confinement knob is the same principle, not an exception to it. Both roles: the no-follow write guarantee binds the viewer receive path too — a hostile peer the box connected to can race a local symlink on its download directory (the same TOCTOU, symmetric), so R-A5's per-write O_NOFOLLOW assert applies to the receive (viewer) path as well as the controlled side, not the controlled side alone.
R-S9MUST Strong permanent password, permanent-only + password approve-mode — no source IP ACL (CPace is the sole gate)
The box exposes no source-IP allow/deny list: like an SSH daemon it binds to all interfaces and relies on authentication, so an inbound peer reaches exactly the mandatory CPace handshake and nothing else. Any IP check would gate only an already-keyed peer — it necessarily runs after CPace, in on_open — so it could never reduce attack surface; it could only deny the authenticated owner by source IP, which §2's bar rejects. CPace is the gate (R-D2). The fork MUST require a non-empty permanent password — non-empty after NFC normalization (R-P1), since CPace has no empty-PRS guard (it derives a valid generator even from b"") — and pin verification-method = use-permanent-password (the one-time-password path is excised — R-X7) and approve-mode = password (not click-to-accept) in the controlled-side policy table (R-S16). The PAKE's PRS is derived from this permanent password (R-P1). Because CPace authenticates from the PRS — a memory-hard salted hash of the password (Argon2id, fixed domain-separation salt, R-P1) — and the legacy validate_password salted-hash check is deleted (R-S2/R-S6), the controlled side MUST hold that PRS at rest — the Argon2id hash, never the plaintext password. It is connect-equivalent (a balanced PAKE keys from it directly, so it is not a one-way verifier) yet it is not the plaintext, so a config read does not surrender a password reused as the OS/sudo login or elsewhere (Appendix C #14). Rotation & liveness: making the credential PRS-usable is a real storage-format change to set_permanent_password (upstream stores h1 = SHA-256(pw‖salt) in two places — the local store and a signed-custom.txt preset in HARD_SETTINGS that validate_password falls through to, R-S16(d)(v); the preset is empty-by-signed-gate on a fork build, so the fork holds one PRS-usable local credential); the responder MUST derive the CPace PRS by a live read of the current at-rest credential on every handshake — no cached PRS, generator input, or derived scalar may outlive a password change — and a change (via --password, R-D2) MUST take effect on the next connection with the old value rejected promptly. Viewer-side twin & distribution: the balanced PAKE needs the same PRS on both ends — but neither end stores the plaintext: each derives the identical Argon2id PRS from the password and the fixed domain-separation salt compiled into both roles (R-P1), so both ends reach the same PRS from the password alone, with nothing per-box to distribute or agree. The viewer's persisted per-peer credential (PeerConfig.password) today caches h1 = SHA-256(pw‖salt) (client.rs), unusable as a CPace PRS — so the fork's "remember password" MUST instead cache the derived Argon2id PRS per peer-address (under the same machine-UUID at-rest wrapper and the same endpoint-compromise caveat — the viewer-side twin of this at-rest change, equally a memory-hard hash and not the plaintext), PRS provisioning onto both ends is a manual out-of-band operator step (the trusted channel R-B2 posits), and a box-password change MUST invalidate the viewer's cached PRS (which simply fails to key until re-provisioned). Per-box independence: each controlled box SHOULD hold an independent permanent password — with independence the password effectively is that box's per-host credential, so an unlimited MITM or a substitute box cannot key without that box's own password; reusing one password across boxes makes them mutually impersonable to a party that already knows the shared password (out of scope, §2). The password is the sole substitution defense — there is no host-key pin behind it, and none is needed within the threat model. The config file MUST therefore be treated as secret by filesystem permissions (its at-rest wrapper is keyed by the machine UUID — obfuscation, not secrecy against a local reader). Balanced-PAKE consequence: the controlled side must hold the PRS to run CPace, so a config read yields a connect-equivalent value — one that can key a session — inherent to a balanced PAKE and acceptable here because endpoint compromise is out of scope (§2); but it is the Argon2id salted hash, not the plaintext, so it is not the user's password for reuse elsewhere and not the OS/sudo credential even when those are reused. An augmented PAKE (e.g. OPAQUE) would let the controlled side store a non-connect-equivalent verifier instead, but reintroduces a stored-verifier and asymmetric initiator/responder model — deliberately rejected for this symmetric, single-user, sovereign design; the Argon2id PRS is the strongest at-rest form available within that balanced, symmetric choice.
R-S10MUST Constant-time secrets; a DoS-safe rate limiter, re-keyed onto the PAKE
Every secret-touching comparison MUST be constant-time (upstream already uses constant_time_eq for the password — MUST NOT regress to ==). The per-IP failure limiter (LOGIN_FAILURES, connection.rs) MUST be kept and re-keyed onto the PAKE, but billed honestly: it is cheap CPU-flood shedding, never credential defense (the strong password + one-guess-per-PAKE is that), so it MUST be tuned loose. Cardinal principle — the limiter must never become the attacker's weapon against the owner: it MUST key on the connecting source and MUST NOT be global or connection-count-wide, so a flood from one source can never lock the legitimate owner (a different source) out of their own machine; and every block MUST decay (see (c)) into a self-healing tarpit, never a permanent ban — a limiter that denies the owner service is doing the attacker's job for them. Direct-IP is the clean case for this: the box keys on the peer's real TCP source address (the accepted socket's addr in the lifted direct_server), not a relay-collapsed one. As inherited the limiter is not DoS-safe, so three coupled fixes are mandatory:
  • (a) Re-key onto the PAKE; gate before the scalar-mult. Inherited, the counter is bumped/checked at three sites, not one — the LoginRequest password path (around validate_password), the Linux headless OS-login path (should_check/should_record_linux_headless_os_auth…, connection.rs), and the terminal OS-login path (check_failure_with_scope(…, FailureScope::TerminalOsLogin)); R-S2 deletes the password path and R-X14/R-X8 excise the other two, so with all three gone the limiter no longer lives at any inherited site — it is reconstituted wholesale at the choke point, keyed on a CPace key-confirmation failure (R-P14c). A failed key-confirmation MUST increment the entry, and the choke point (server.rs, peer IP in hand) MUST check the threshold and reject before running the PAKE, shedding floods ahead of the scalar-mult. This is the limiter's real job — cheap CPU-flood shedding, not credential defense: the strong password (R-S9) plus one-guess-per-PAKE is the structural brute-force defense, so the limiter may be tuned loose. Two implementation notes: the limiter is a Connection method keyed on self.ip, but the choke point has no Connection yet — it MUST be lifted to a free function taking the raw peer IP; and only a CPace key-confirmation failure may count (a wrong password or a MITM), never a transport/parse abort, so handshake noise cannot inflate the counter.
  • (b) Bound the memory. Verified: LOGIN_FAILURES only ever grows — an entry is removed only on a successful login; no size cap, no LRU, no time eviction — and it stores a per-full-address entry even for IPv6 (it blocks by /64–/48 prefix yet still inserts the full address). An attacker owning one IPv6 /64 (264 addresses) thus grows it without bound — a memory-exhaustion DoS — and counting pre-PAKE widens that to cheap pre-auth garbage. The fork MUST bound the structure (a hard size cap — e.g. ≤ 8192 entries — plus LRU/time eviction; the inherited store is a never-evicted [Arc<Mutex<HashMap<String,(i32,i32,i32)>>>; 2] — a pair of maps, password and 2FA paths — at connection.rs) and key IPv6 by prefix (≤ /64), never the full address. On the v4-only box (R-D5) this entire IPv6-prefix subsystem is dead code — get_ipv6_prefixes returns None for v4 peers, and try_into_v4 (called at connection.rs, defined in hbb_common/src/lib.rs) normalizes v4-mapped addresses first — so the limiter collapses to a bounded, decaying, per-v4-address counter and the /64 amplification vector cannot occur; drop the prefix machinery on that build.
  • (c) Decay the block; never lock out permanently. Verified: the cumulative block (failure.2 > 30 per IP, > 100 per /48) is a lifetime counter that never decays — once tripped, that source/prefix is blocked until a successful login or a restart. That is a permanent-lockout weapon against any shared egress (CGNAT or other shared mobile/NAT egress) or a shared /48. The fork MUST window/decay the counter (a tarpit that expires) so legitimate and collateral sources self-heal. The inherited thresholds are cumulative, never-decaying lifetime counts — >30 per IP, >60 per /64, >80 per /56, >100 per /48 (connection.rs) — atop a per-minute burst shed (>6/min); keep the per-minute shed, but make the cumulative count decay (e.g. clear an entry after ~60 min with no new failure) so it is a tarpit, not a lifetime ban.
Topology note: the controlled box keys on the connecting source IP, and the legitimate viewer almost always connects from a different source than an attacker (and TCP source cannot be spoofed through a full handshake) — so the common case is an attacker throttling only itself; (b) and (c) defang the residual shared-prefix/CGNAT collateral. Direct-IP mode is the clean case for per-IP limiting — the peer's real address is visible (no relay IP-collapsing), so a bounded per-IP counter is genuinely meaningful there; conversely, if ever deployed behind IP-collapsing infrastructure (a relay), add an application-level identity to the failure key. Observability gap to close: upstream emits no log line on a failed login or a rate-limit block (the wrong-password branch only bumps the counter and sends a protobuf error; post_alarm_audit is dead with no api-server), so the fork MUST emit a structured key-confirmation-failure record (source IP + outcome) at the choke point — the only audit signal on a serverless box, and the prerequisite for an optional host-level fail2ban backstop, which (being post-hoc and log-driven) complements but cannot replace the in-process pre-PAKE shed.
R-S11MUST The local IPC and service are not bypasses; least privilege
The UI⇄service IPC MUST stay local-only with its parent-dir hardening intact; no network peer may reach session functionality without the PAKE. The controlled-side service MUST run least-privilege (failure mode C). The daemon IPC handler is itself a config-integrity boundary: the inherited main channel wrote any config key via Data::Options→set_options, the permanent password via Data::Config, and the whole config via Data::SyncConfig with no allowlist (ipc.rs) — so a local process could reverse the §8 excisions and §9 hard-pins from inside. The fork MUST allowlist the keys writable over IPC and reject trust-anchor / credential / hard-pin writes there (the R-S16-pinned keys are already rejected at is_option_can_save per R-S16(c), so the allowlist need only cover the residual non-pinned keys; the inherited world-facing _service socket narrowed itself to SyncConfig, but R-S11b/R-S11c forbid that too because whole-config service IPC is still the wrong authority boundary). On Windows that main pipe is created allow_everyone_create and accepts any same-session process (ipc.rs, auth.rs) — the fork MUST also tighten it to a same-user DACL. The allowlist MUST be applied per write-arm, not just to the set_options key set: the inherited main channel also exposed Data::Socks → set_socks (ipc.rs, a proxy that re-routes all traffic — a local MITM primitive an Options-key allowlist would miss), the salt / unlock-pin / id+set_key_confirmed(false) sub-keys of Data::Config, and Data::RemoveTrustedDevices/ClearTrustedDevices. Make it a positive chokepoint, not a denylist: route every IPC config write through a single allowlist function and CI/AST-assert that no Config::set*/set_socks/set_permanent_password is reachable from the main-channel handler except through it — so the handler cannot write a key that is not on the list, rather than the spec having to enumerate every dangerous arm. Beyond config writes, two action classes on the main channel escape a key-allowlist and MUST be handled separately: (i) the mediator-control arms Data::TestRendezvousServer (ipc.rs → test_rendezvous_server, an outbound probe) and Data::Deployed (RendezvousMediator::restart) write no config key, so the allowlist never sees them — they MUST be removed with the mediator (R-D4) or gated out of the handler; and (ii) the RAII Drop guards CheckTestNatType (constructed at ipc.rs, and in set_socks/set_options) and CheckIfRestart, whose Drop fires test_nat_type() (binding an ephemeral STUN UDP socket — an egress IOC, R-D6) / restart() at arm entry, before any allowlist check runs. An allowlist cannot stop a Drop that constructs on entry, so the fork MUST make the trigger unreachable instead: pin socks and allow-websocket immutable (R-D6) so CheckTestNatType's is_direct can never change between construction and drop, and excise CheckTestNatType/CheckIfRestart with the mediator (R-D4). R-A6's reachability guard MUST traverse Drop impls reachable from the handler — a name-only grep misses them. (Upstream 1.4.7 already gates the IPC transport — per-uid 0600 sockets + SO_PEERCRED — so this is the residual write-authorization gap, not the socket itself.)
R-S11aMUST Executable-match is never the sole IPC authorization; fresh credentials; no /tmp adoption
Verified: the cross-uid IPC accept gate combines a kernel-attested peer credential (SO_PEERCRED uid: root-or-active-user, auth.rs) with an executable-path match (/proc/<pid>/exe equals the service binary, auth.rs). The exe-match alone is not an authorization — any local user may run the genuine root-owned /usr/bin/rustdesk and satisfy it — so it is only sound behind the uid gate, and CI MUST AST-assert that no accept path reaches the exe-match without a preceding uid check (individually weak, jointly sound; a refactor must not drop the uid gate and leave exe-match standing). Two further hardenings the inherited code leaves open: (a) the _service channel MUST authorize against a fresh active-user lookup (as the _uinput_* path already does, uinput.rs), not the service-loop cache, so a just-switched-out user cannot pass in the cache-lag window; and (b) the service MUST NOT adopt a pre-existing world-traversable /tmp/{app}-service it did not create — on owner mismatch it MUST reject-and-recreate (no fchown-adopt), and a startup assertion (R-A4) MUST confirm the parent dir is 0o0711 root-owned with each socket at its expected mode before listening. (The world-mode 0o0666 on the _service/_uinput_* sockets is a deliberate consequence of the root-service ↔ user---server cross-uid design, mediated by these gates — not a permission bug; Appendix B. On the §17 Xorg build the _uinput_* sockets are compiled out entirely — R-X13, since uinput is the Wayland input path — leaving only _service world-mode there.)
R-S11bMUST Service-owned unattended credentials and machine policy are privileged operations, never ordinary IPC config writes
Authority follows enforcement. The process that will honor a remote-access credential or enforce a machine-wide policy is the only process that may store or mutate it. In installed service mode the unattended permanent password/PRS, its salt, any host identity or trust store, service reachability policy, session-switch policy, and privileged OS actions are owned by the root/SYSTEM/LaunchDaemon service, not by the Flutter UI, tray, same-session process, or user-owned config file. A user-session process may collect input and request a change, but it may not commit it through Data::Config, Data::Options, Data::SyncConfig, or any whole-config import. Read-shaped IPC/server metadata paths likewise may not create, import, or persist service-owned identity or salt material: salt reads return only an already-stored salt, and salt creation belongs only to explicit password provisioning. Because this fork is direct-IP only and carries no per-box identity, new configs MUST NOT auto-generate a numeric RustDesk ID; existing stored IDs may be read only as inert compatibility metadata, config load/store may not migrate or re-encrypt them, and a login username must be a direct address rather than a numeric-ID fallback. Changing the unattended password is therefore a privileged machine-administration operation: Linux MUST use a specific polkit/admin-authorized service action; Windows MUST use UAC/service-admin authorization or an equivalent SCM/service-owned proof; Windows service-owned session-sharing policy likewise MUST be a typed service action committed by the LocalSystem service, not a UI-side registry or shell write; macOS MUST use the privileged-helper/Authorization Services path. The accepted message is a narrow typed operation such as SetUnattendedPassword, validated and performed by the service after OS authorization; it is not a whitelisted key in the normal IPC config bus. Non-installed user-mode is the only case where the current user may own the permanent password directly, because the same user-owned process is then the one honoring it. This closes the local-to-remote escalation class where an unprivileged local caller mints or replaces the credential a privileged unattended service will later accept over the network.
R-S11cMUST Privileged IPC is a typed capability API; the receiver validates the target and authority
No privileged process may trust caller-side validation, same-session status, same-UID status, or executable-path match as the authority for a privileged action. The service MUST validate the full operation at the receiving end: Windows session switching may not accept a raw caller-supplied UserSid; SAS/HKLM-touching actions may not be generic _service commands; Linux/macOS _service may not exchange whole Config/Config2 with the active user; CM/file/audio/whiteboard helper IPC may not rely on "same UID" for connection ownership. Cross-process helpers that perform file, audio, display, input, terminal, whiteboard, or service actions MUST receive a per-connection capability/nonce minted by the owning connection and tied to the authorized AuthConnType (R-S19). Pre-login file operations are forbidden. The service side validates target session existence, policy state, requested action, and capability freshness itself; a UI or connection process can request but cannot prove policy by saying it already checked. This is the positive replacement for an ambient internal bus: local IPC remains a transport, but every authority-changing message is narrow, typed, receiver-authorized, and fail-closed. Windows terminal principal: a service-owned LocalSystem server MUST derive the served session from its own process token and obtain that session user's primary token before Terminal authorization succeeds; persistent reconnect identity MUST bind token session ID, user SID, and logon-session AuthenticationId, while the entry retains its original owned primary token and revalidates its token-object identity before launch. Every operation MUST use an exclusive entry-identity, attachment generation, and authority epoch rather than a bare service ID. Reservation MUST NOT mutate an existing persistent service before activation; current authority MUST be checkpointed before a checked login-response write, and staged state plus output subscription MUST commit only after that write succeeds. Shell creation MUST use an epoch-bound opening reservation and occur outside the service lock; input and resize sinks MUST linearize against the epoch and never block on a full channel. Logoff or served-principal replacement MUST revoke the token, openings, and shells even while detached, using session-event plus periodic entry-owned validation; attached connections MUST close on revocation. The Windows helper pipes MUST be non-overlapped, local-only, first-instance, directionally ACLed to SYSTEM plus the exact token logon SID, and bound to the retained helper process object, PID, and token principal. The helper MUST receive a non-inherited environment and validated profile directory from that same token with no service-context fallback, and MUST be assigned while suspended to a kill-on-close job before it can create the ConPTY shell. A service-owned/LocalSystem direct PTY without the session token MUST fail again at the launch sink; a direct non-LocalSystem server may use only its process owner.
R-S12MUST No insecure build/runtime configuration is reachable
No feature flag, env var, config key, or CLI flag may disable/weaken the PAKE, the encryption, the no-follow write guarantee (R-S8), or the §9 assertions. Such controls MUST be removed, not defaulted off. For the controlled side's security-policy options specifically, R-S16 is the realization of this principle: the policy is a compile-time table the Config::get_option funnel returns and the is_option_can_save guard rejects, so no key can be defaulted-permissive or written back on.
R-S13MUST The initiator (viewer) is held to the responder's bar
§1 puts both roles in scope; this requirement governs the initiator (viewer) role — present on every box, since any box can connect out, including the exposed jump-host that views an inner box (§17) — whose connecting side must meet the same keyed-before-any-message guarantee as the responder (§4.4); the inherited initiator path does not, and keying the responder (R-S1) does not fix it. (A box running purely as --server never initiates, so this surface is unreachable there; (a)–(d) are how the initiator path closes it whenever a box views.) The initiator path MUST: (a) delete the unkeyed direct-IP / domain:port early-returns (client.rs) and route every initiation — direct, rendezvous, relay — through the CPace initiator handshake (R-P14), so a usable Stream is never returned without a confirmed PAKE; (b) assert is_secured() before the viewer dispatches or tunnels any byte — promoting the inherited informational check (io_loop.rs, today only a UI lock-icon) to a fail-closed abort, the initiator analog of R-A1; (c) decode only the dedicated Cpace message pre-key on the client path (parse_from_bytes::<Cpace>, replacing the full-union Message::parse_from_bytes at client.rs), and call set_max_packet_length to the small pre-PAKE bound before the first read (R-S7); and (d) connect TCP-only — gating mod kcp_stream out (R-D4) and removing the udp_nat_connect punch path (client.rs) so the kcp-sys C parser is absent in this role too. Residual (universal; documented, not closable by keying): a box deliberately connected to a hostile but password-correct peer feeds attacker bytes to the in-process native decoders (§4.4); this is bounded operationally by the deep-link confirmation and channel restriction of R-X6, not by the PAKE (Appendix C #2b), and the fork SHOULD close it by sandboxing the decode path. The TCP-only choice costs UDP NAT traversal — acceptable for the static-IP direct deployment (§17), where connecting-by-address (R-F3) is preserved over TCP.
R-S14MUST Screen capture is bound to a PAKE-authenticated session — a reused grant must not capture outside one
The security boundary on screen capture is the PAKE (R-S1/R-A1), not the OS consent dialog — a distinction that matters because this fork targets unattended access (R-S9). On Android, MediaProjection consent is a system dialog the user must physically tap and which cannot be auto-granted, so the inherited token reuse (MainService keeps the projection alive across session end via reuseVirtualDisplay/setSurface(null), MainService.kt) avoids a re-consent tap per connection where the OS permits it — bounded, since the projection is owned by the foreground service (acquired on start, and released with the VirtualDisplay, ImageReader, and Surface on service teardown — R-D7a) and Android-14 per-session re-consent and Android-15 auto-stop-on-lock cap what unattended reuse achieves. The requirement is not re-consent but binding: capture MUST begin only inside a confirmed PAKE session — no held grant (the Android projection token, or the Wayland xdg-portal restore-token at libs/scrap/…/wayland/pipewire.rs) may drive capture pre-authentication, and the held grant MUST NOT be reachable by any non-authenticated path. The persisted Wayland restore-token is a local at-rest exposure (a local reader could lift it) in the same §2 endpoint-compromise-out-of-scope class as the UUID-obfuscated config (Appendix C #14); the fork SHOULD encrypt it at rest and release the projection when the service is not running (and on the §17 Xorg build, R-X12 compiles the Wayland capture path out entirely, making this half absent by construction). (Re-prompting per session MUST NOT be added where it would break unattended access. The §17 Xorg box has no capture-consent gate at all — it captures for the authenticated peer by design, the intended unattended behavior — so this requirement is about where the gate is: the PAKE, not an OS dialog.) macOS source: the held-grant twin is the persistent TCC.db Screen-Recording/Accessibility grant — there is no fork-stored token (so the "encrypt at rest" half is N/A), but the grant is app-wide because Release.entitlements ships the App Sandbox off, held by a KeepAlive, login-window-resident --server. Capture is already PAKE-bound on macOS through the shared lazy-on-subscribe path, so the obligations are to keep that binding, minimize the entitlement set (the full per-key allow-list spans Release.entitlements, Profile.entitlements, and Debug.entitlements; cs.allow-jit is debug-only, while Profile and Release are AOT and carry no JIT exception), and ensure a --server restart re-enters capture only through a fresh PAKE session (the macOS analog of Android's START_NOT_STICKY). iOS: no capture, so N/A.
R-S15MUST The viewer's in-session message handling is a config-integrity boundary — a keyed hostile peer may not write the viewer's PeerConfig
§1 puts the initiator in scope and R-S13 keys it (whenever a box views; a box running purely as --server performs no client dispatch, so this surface is unreachable there) — but keying is where the spec stopped: a correctly-keyed but hostile peer (one the viewer was induced to connect to, §2/§4.4) then sends ordinary in-session messages that reach the viewer's persistent PeerConfig. Verified: Misc::BackNotification→PrivacyModeState wrote the peer's unbounded impl_key string under privacy-mode-impl-key (io_loop.rs) with no user click, and could also flip the saved privacy-mode boolean without proving the viewer requested a privacy transition; TerminalResponse.service_id and the peer's PeerInfo username/hostname/platform are persisted on every connect (client.rs) — all via save_config; and PeerInfo.version/platform formerly selected or rewrote the persisted PeerConfig.keyboard_mode. This is the initiator twin of R-S11, whose config-write allowlist is written IPC-only and never inspects the client dispatch. The fork MUST extend R-S11's single-allowlist chokepoint to io_loop.rs: route every peer-message-driven config write through a validated allowlist (privacy-mode-impl-key rejected unless it is in the compile-time get_supported_privacy_mode_impl() set; PrivacyModeState persistence admitted only when it matches a receiver-owned pending local outbound TogglePrivacyMode or legacy OptionMessage.privacy_mode request for the default remote-control session; PeerInfo strings length-bounded; peer-derived keyboard-mode compatibility computed only as runtime fallback and never persisted), and CI/AST-assert (R-A6) that no Config::set*/save_config is reachable from a peer-message arm except through it. (Severity is config-poisoning, not RCE — the impl map is compile-time and keyboard mode is enum-bounded — but it is the persistent-write class the §4.4 audit found that survives a correctly-keyed peer, so it is closed, not accepted as the §2 native-decoder residual is.)
R-S16MUST The controlled side enforces ONE compile-time policy at the config funnel — never a permissive default, a peer/server field, or a runtime write
Principle. The controlled side draws every security decision from exactly two input classes: config options — almost all resolved through the funnel Config::get_option (hbb_common/src/config.rs → get_or; precedence OVERWRITE_SETTINGS → config-file → DEFAULT_SETTINGS → ""), with a handful in a second namespace read via get_builtin_option (closed in (d)(iv)) — and peer/rendezvous protobuf (the control_permissions blob and the LoginRequest credential fields). RustDesk makes every one of these fail open: option2bool returns true for any unset enable-* key (config.rs), verification_method() → UseBothPasswords and approve_mode() → Both on empty (password_security.rs), and permission() honors a rendezvous-server-supplied bit before any local policy (connection.rs). Policy MUST therefore be a property of the artifact — a compile-time constant the program enforces unconditionally — not a default that can be unset, wrong, peer-supplied, or locally overwritten. The security resolvers are left unmodified (verification_method()/approve_mode()/Permission::terminal are not rewritten to return a hand-rolled constant): the funnel pin makes them return the policy transparently — a single-edit closure rather than a per-call-site rewrite.
  • (a) One source of truth. A pub const PINNED_SETTINGS: &[(&str, &str)] in config.rs — the idiom RustDesk already uses for RS_PUB_KEY/RENDEZVOUS_PORT (config.rs) — unconditional: the fork is one build and is always hardened, so the policy is enforced by every artifact, never behind a feature flag. It pins only Config server-settings keys (KEYS_SETTINGS); the parallel LocalConfig map (viewer UI prefs) is untouched. The table — opinionated and exact — is the box's entire security policy in one auditable place: 
    verification-method = use-permanent-password   # was: unset → UseBothPasswords (accept either credential)
approve-mode        = password                 # was: unset → Both (silent click-to-accept)
access-mode         = full                     # complete control for the authenticated owner — the one mode
enable-keyboard=Y  enable-clipboard=Y  enable-file-transfer=Y  enable-audio=Y  enable-camera=Y   # the content channels a remote-control box needs (§4.3)
enable-terminal=Y  enable-tunnel=Y             # full control for the authenticated owner (R-X8, R-D6)
enable-remote-restart=Y  enable-record-session=Y  enable-block-input=Y  enable-privacy-mode=Y  enable-virtual-display=N
                                               # the rest of full control — granted to the authenticated owner
allow-remote-config-modification=Y             # the owner manages RustDesk itself from the session (R-S16)
enable-trusted-devices=N  2fa=  bot=           # no 2FA-bypass, no TOTP, no Telegram push (R-X7, R-D6)
api-server=  custom-rendezvous-server=  relay-server=  proxy-url=  proxy-username=  proxy-password=  key= # egress-silent; no proxy credentials or trust-anchor override (R-D6/R-S11b)
allow-websocket=N  allow-insecure-tls-fallback=N  allow-linux-headless=N  # R-D6, R-X14
stop-service=N  allow-only-conn-window-open=                              # service un-killable by a local write (R-X9); never self-DoS a headless box
    Every capability the authenticated owner exercises is Y — full control is the box's single mode (§17 sovereignty: the CPace peer is the trusted owner who already holds the OS/sudo password, §2); the only Ns left are the sovereignty pins — egress-empty, no 2FA, no insecure transport — a different class entirely (R-D6/R-X7/R-X14), while empty pins also remove stored egress endpoints, proxy credentials, and the legacy trust-anchor override. An operator who needs a different policy edits the const and rebuilds (the R-F4 build-time-choice discipline) — never a runtime knob. (direct-server/direct-access-port are not here: R-D4 makes the listener unconditional and R-F4 pins the port as a code literal, not an option.)
  • (b) Pin the read funnel. At the head of Config::get_option (config.rs), before the get_or lookup, return the pinned value if the key is in PINNED_SETTINGS. This makes every config-driven resolver return the policy with no edit to any of them — verification_method(), approve_mode(), option2bool("enable-*") inside is_permission_enabled_locally (connection.rs), is_headless_allowed(), the egress reads — and covers every un-enumerated KEYS_SETTINGS security key for free, because that funnel is singular for the KEYS_SETTINGS namespace — a second option-read funnel (get_builtin_option, the KEYS_BUILDIN_SETTINGS namespace) exists and is closed in (d)(iv). The whole-map read is the same funnel: Config::get_options MUST overlay PINNED_SETTINGS last before returning an effective option map to the UI, CLI, or IPC Data::Options(None) cache, so broad reads cannot observe a stale default/stored/signed-custom value for a pinned key and cannot hide a pinned-empty key as "absent." Strictly stronger than RustDesk's own OVERWRITE_SETTINGS, which is a runtime RwLock populated only if a RustDesk-Ed25519-signed custom.txt loads (common.rs) — a fork cannot sign it, and it can be left empty or mutated in-process; a const can be neither.
  • (c) Pin the write funnel. In is_option_can_save (config.rs — the single guard already gating both set_option and set_options/purify_options), return false for any pinned key, reusing the existing drop-and-purge-from-the-persisted-file behavior. A local process, an IPC Data::Options/SyncConfig write (R-S11), or a server-pushed config-merge (R-X3) thus cannot set a pinned option key — structurally, with no allowlist lookup for this subset. Scope: this guards only the options map; credential, salt, legacy identity, and unlock-pin state are struct fields, not options, so any IPC Data::Config arm that writes them and any whole-struct Data::SyncConfig import bypass is_option_can_save entirely. Those writes are R-S11's per-arm allowlist job, not this guard's.
  • (d) Close the two gates that bypass get_option. (i) permission() (connection.rs) consults the rendezvous-server control_permissions blob before local policy. The mediator is excised (R-D4) so it is always None (rendezvous_mediator.rs) and (b) governs the fall-through — but the fork MUST also add an early return Self::is_permission_enabled_locally(opt) at the head of permission() that skips the control_permissions branch entirely, closing the server-push vector by construction, not merely by the mediator's absence. (The #[cfg(feature="flutter")] access-mode shortcut in is_permission_enabled_locally is present — the one binary ships flutter — so the access-mode = full pin (a) governs it: the grant-everything shortcut returns true for every capability, with the explicit enable-* = Y pins as the belt behind it — any path that consults an enable-* directly still resolves to grant.) (ii) The local CM ipc::Data::SwitchPermission handler (connection.rs) reassigns conn.keyboard/clipboard/audio/file/… at runtime, bypassing permission(); once the policy is fixed a runtime re-widener has no place, so the handler arm MUST be removed (the headless --service has no CM to drive it anyway; structural, not merely covered by R-S11's allowlist). The peer's inbound disable_* overlays (connection.rs) only ever restrict the cached booleans, so they need no change. (iii) The SOCKS/proxy state is the one structured config that bypasses the string-option funnel: get_socks() reads OVERWRITE_SETTINGS/CONFIG2.socks/DEFAULT_SETTINGS directly (config.rs) and get_network_type() likewise, while set_socks writes the structured CONFIG2.socks field — so neither (b)'s read pin nor (c)'s write guard reaches it. The fork MUST therefore make get_socks()/get_network_type() return None/Direct and set_socks() inert at those accessors directly; the table pins proxy-url and the proxy credential keys for any string-option reader, but that is not sufficient alone because the structured field bypasses the string map. This keeps CheckTestNatType's is_direct constant so the IPC Drop STUN-probe never fires (R-D6/R-S11). (iv) A second option-read funnel exists outside Config::get_option: get_builtin_option (common.rs) reads the BUILTIN_SETTINGS map for the KEYS_BUILDIN_SETTINGS namespace (config.rs), which the (b) pin does not reach. The security-relevant builtin keys read on the controlled path are one-way-file-transfer (connection.rs — the directionality of an authenticated peer's file pull), allow-logon-screen-password (feeding the approve_mode==Click decision), one-way-clipboard-redirection, and enable-perm-change-in-accept-window (ui_cm_interface.rs). BUILTIN_SETTINGS is populated only from the same RustDesk-signed custom.txt a fork cannot produce, so on a fork build it is empty and these take their safe defaults — not a fail-open reachable by a non-password-knower — but it is a real second funnel the pin must account for. The fork MUST (1) assert BUILTIN_SETTINGS is empty at startup (R-A4 — no managed override leaked in), and (2) if it ever pins a KEYS_BUILDIN_SETTINGS value (e.g. one-way-file-transfer=Y to make the root box push-only), mirror the PINNED_SETTINGS consult into get_builtin_option too — the Config::get_option pin alone does nothing for these keys.
