A naïvely-implemented transport hardening on a DMZ box is a vulnerability in the costume of a fix; three rules follow and are normative throughout §20. (1) Flood-safe observability. Any log or metric emitted on an attacker-reachable hot path — a shed connection, a rate-limit block, a key-confirmation failure, an accept error — MUST be rate-limited or aggregated; a per-event log line is itself a log/disk-amplification DoS under the very flood it reports. The required form is a lock-light counter bumped per event and a single summary emitted at most once per fixed interval (with the most-recent source for a hint), never one line per event. (2) Shed cheaply, early. A connection that will be rejected (a capacity shed or a rate-limit block) MUST be dropped before it consumes any contended resource — before a task is spawned, before any global/per-connection lock is taken, before any allocation — so rejecting a flood costs accept+close, not spawn+lock+handshake; a control that sheds after taking the resource it is meant to protect is no protection. (3) Zero normal-path cost. Each control MUST add only negligible, contention-free overhead to a legitimate connection (a lock-free atomic, not a global mutex; no allocation on the steady-state path), so the defense never taxes the owner. R-A8's owner-safety test (R-S10) and the R-B4 socket-surface smoke-test MUST exercise these properties — that a flood is shed without the shedder itself amplifying load, and that a legitimate connection under a concurrent flood still completes.

20.1 Resource exhaustion — the unauthenticated DMZ flood

R-T1MUST Bound concurrent connections so an unauthenticated flood cannot exhaust the host — reconciled with R-S10's cardinal rule
The critical DMZ exposure. A serverless accept loop that spawns one task per accepted socket with no bound lets an unauthenticated attacker open connections faster than they complete — each holding an fd and a task through the two ≤18 s CPace read-steps — until the host exhausts file descriptors or memory. The per-source guess limiter (R-S10) bounds confirmation-failure rate, not connection count: an attacker who merely opens sockets (or sends a valid step-① and stalls) never trips it. Absent a self-enforced memory/task ceiling this is host-fatal, not service-fatal — the flood allocates until the host OOM-kills, taking the whole box down — and a one-line connection flood is then the no-password, no-firewall-needed exploit, precisely the "defensible even with no firewall" property R-D3 promises. The fork MUST bound it in two layers, each implemented to the §20.0 discipline. (a) A self-enforced OS ceiling in the binary. The --server process MUST apply its own hard limits at startup, before the accept loop, so the bound holds under any launcher (systemd, a per-user supervisor, a bare container — R-D8), never only under a particular unit file: a per-process setrlimit(RLIMIT_NOFILE) that lowers only the soft limit far below the inherited default while preserving the inherited hard ceiling (legitimate single-user use is a handful of sessions, so a high soft fd ceiling only serves an attacker or a descriptor leak; the hard ceiling is kept so a child the --server spawns — notably the owner's full-access terminal, R-F1 — can raise its own soft via ulimit -n, because a resource bound must not become a privilege confinement of the owner's shell, R-D3a) and a hard global cap on concurrent authenticated sessions (rejected at on_open past MAX_AUTHED_SESSIONS — the post-key population the pre-key semaphore in (b) does not cover). Together with the per-session §4.2 memory bounds and the (b) pre-key bound, these cap the process's fds, tasks, and live memory so a flood or a leak is service-fatal, not host-fatal, under any launcher. (There is no clean per-process RSS ceiling to self-apply via setrlimit: RLIMIT_AS bounds virtual address space, not resident memory, so a host-relative AS cap would break a legitimate large-virtual / low-RSS process; RLIMIT_NPROC is per-UID, so under a per-user supervisor it would bound the entire desktop stack, not the service. The RSS/task ceiling is therefore the cgroup's job — the redundant outer bound below.) The shipped systemd unit (res/rustdesk.service) SHOULD additionally carry the matching cgroup ceilings as a redundant outer bound, not the sole enforcement: TasksMax, host-relative MemoryHigh/MemoryMax (so the service OOM-kills inside its own cgroup before the host does), LimitNOFILE, and Restart=on-failure+RestartSec for self-recovery (which fires on a cgroup OOM-kill but not a clean systemctl stop, so it cannot loop). (b) An in-process bound on concurrent pre-key handshakes — a counting semaphore acquired with a non-blocking try-acquire (never a queue — queuing re-creates the backlog the bound exists to shed), the permit held only across the handshake and released the instant keying hands off to the session, so the bound caps the cheap, attacker-reachable half-open population, not legitimate long-lived authenticated sessions. Per §20.0 rule 2 the slot MUST be acquired in the accept path before the connection task is spawned and before any per-connection server lock (e.g. the connection-id allocation) is taken, so a shed connection costs accept+close, not spawn+lock+handshake; per §20.0 rule 1 the shed MUST be reported through a rate-limited aggregate, never a per-shed log line. Reconciliation with R-S10's cardinal principle (load-bearing): R-S10 forbids a "global or connection-count-wide" limiter because a single-source flood could then lock the owner (a different source) out. The (b) semaphore is a global count, so it MUST be a capacity shed, never a per-source ban: the budget MUST be generous enough that an owner's lone connection essentially always finds a slot under any non-saturating load; it MUST drop the newest excess (an owner's in-flight handshake, already holding a permit, is never evicted); and a per-source-IP connection cap MUST NOT be the primary control — a CGNAT-shared attacker on the owner's egress IP could fill the owner's per-IP slots (R-S10's named scenario) — and if added at all MUST be generous, time-decaying, and subordinate to the global drop-on-full. The preferred shape is source-agnostic accept-rate limiting (a token bucket, which also damps the accept-and-drop CPU spin under a sustained flood) plus the global pre-key semaphore — shedding excess rather than banning a source, "a tarpit, not a lifetime ban" (R-S10(c)) on the connection-count axis. Only an R-P3 key-confirmation failure may ever feed the source-keyed limiter (R-P14c, unchanged).

20.2 Cancellation safety — futures dropped mid-operation

Why this is the hard part

The controlled-side run loop (connection.rs) is a tokio::select! mixing cancellation-sensitive reads (conn.stream.next()), channel recv()s, and writes inside branch bodies (conn.stream.send(…).await), plus timeout(_, send()) wrappers (tcp.rs send_bytes). select! drops the unchosen branch futures every poll; a dropped write or a half-updated invariant is where a stream protocol silently corrupts. The async runtime makes this safe only incidentally: tokio's select! short-circuits on the first matching Ready (it never polls a future to Ready and then discards it), and tokio-util's Framed persists its read and write buffers across a dropped poll — so a loop whose every call site behaves is cancellation-safe. But that is an emergent property of every call site, not a property of construction. The requirements below MUST convert it into an enforced invariant, because on a transport this central a single careless edit must not be one line away from a silent crypto-stream desync.

R-T2MUST A send error or write timeout is fatal-to-the-connection; a keyed stream is structurally poisoned, never reused
send_bytes (tcp.rs) wraps the framed send() in timeout(self.3, …) and the controlled stream always has a write timeout set (SEND_TIMEOUT_VIDEO=12 s / SEND_TIMEOUT_OTHER=120 s, connection.rs), so the write future is droppable mid-flush on deadline. The secretbox seal pre-increments write_seq (cpace.rs) before the bytes reach send_bytes; tokio-util's FramedWrite keeps the unflushed ciphertext in its persistent write buffer. This is harmless only while every send-error path tears the connection down (on_close+break) and the FramedStream — which owns the cipher and the half-flushed buffer — is dropped with it; that safety is a call-site convention, not an invariant. The instant any future edit does if let Err(_) = stream.send(x).await { log; /* keep looping */ }, the next send re-flushes the stale buffered prefix and then seals a new frame under a fresh nonce with the old bytes still queued ahead — a permanent c2s wire/counter desync (and, worst case, a torn frame). The fork MUST make poisoning structural: on any send/recv Err, mark the FramedStream poisoned (e.g. take the inner Framed out of an Option, or a poisoned flag) so every subsequent send_bytes/next short-circuits to Err/None and the connection dies fail-closed. Equivalently and more cleanly, MUST remove per-write timeouts on the encrypted stream entirely and rely on a single connection-level liveness deadline driven by a timer branch in the select! (the loop already has test_delay_timer/auto_disconnect_timer) — a liveness timer never drops a send mid-flush, so the write buffer can never straddle a consumed nonce. (On Windows a wedged TCP send blocks longer before back-pressure surfaces, so this deadline MUST be a select! timer arm, not SO_SNDTIMEO, to fire while poll_flush is Pending.)
R-T3MUST The reader and control channels stay pollable during a write — a dedicated writer task, never send().await in a select branch
Writes execute inside select! branch bodies (conn.stream.send(&value).await, connection.rs), and a branch body runs to completion before the loop re-enters its poll. So while flushing one large video frame (up to the 32 MiB session cap) on a slow/back-pressured peer, no other branch is polled: not the inbound conn.stream.next() (a peer disconnect/close is not observed), not rx_from_cm.recv() (a CM Data::Close/operator teardown is not observed), not the liveness timer — for up to the 12–120 s write-timeout window. This is a real liveness/teardown-latency defect (a peer-initiated or operator-initiated shutdown is honoured only after a multi-second write drains), DoS-relevant on an exposed box. The fork MUST restructure to the "actor owns the socket" pattern: Framed::split() (or hold the write half behind an mpsc), spawn a dedicated writer task that is the sole consumer of the sink, fed by an mpsc of already-sealed frames; the main loop then enqueues (non-blocking) and continues polling reads/CM/timers, never await-ing a write. Ordering invariant (so this stays cancellation- and nonce-correct): seal (which advances write_seq) MUST happen on the single-producer enqueue side so the nonce advances in exact channel-FIFO order, and the writer task MUST be the only consumer (single-writer, R-T8) so flush order = seal order = wire order. Back-pressure is then a bounded writer channel whose full condition is the liveness signal to drop the connection (replacing the per-write timeout of R-T2).
R-T4MUST All per-connection cleanup is cancellation-safe — RAII/Drop, never post-loop code
When a connection task is dropped (peer gone, a parent select! aborting it, runtime shutdown), the run-loop future is dropped at its current .await and any straight-line code after the loop never runs. Cleanup splits across two classes: the RAII half is correct (ALIVE_CONNS, AUTHED_CONNS, CONTROL_PERMISSIONS_ARRAY, encoder removal, wake-lock/wallpaper/resolution restore are all Drop impls on guards owned by the future frame — they run on cancellation); but the post-loop half (connection.rs) is lost on cancellation — notably privacy-mode-off / screen-unblank (a dropped session can leave the physical console blanked — a local-security regression on the box), Server::remove_connection (the Server's own map diverges from the RAII-pruned global), the capture-subscription unsub, and the CM Data::Close notification (the connection-manager is never told the session ended). Worse, the CM child process (run_me(["--cm"])) is a plain std::process::Child pushed to a detached global CHILD_PROCESS Vec (server.rs) — not kill_on_drop, not owned by the connection — so a cancelled connection leaks its CM child until it independently notices its IPC socket died. The fork MUST make the rule absolute: every per-connection resource and external notification is released via Drop/an owned guard, never via code after the loop. Concretely: fold the post-loop tail (privacy-off, remove_connection, capture-unsub, cursor-record-stop) into the connection's Drop path (note tx_to_cm.send(Close) on an unbounded sender is synchronous and can run in Drop, closing the CM-notify gap); spawn the CM child via tokio Command with .kill_on_drop(true) and store the tokio::process::Child in a connection-owned guard, not the detached global; and for work that is genuinely async (a final lock_screen().await), drive the loop with an explicit CancellationToken branch that runs an async drain on graceful shutdown (R-T9) with the RAII guard as the hard-drop backstop.
R-T5MUST Decode+decrypt is atomic and the recv nonce advances only on a delivered frame — fold decryption into the Decoder
FramedStream::next (tcp.rs) does self.0.next().await (framed decode) then key.dec(bytes), which advances read_seq. This is cancellation-safe only incidentally, by two runtime facts: (1) tokio's select! short-circuits on the first matching Ready, so a next() that completes (decrypting + advancing the counter) is always the chosen branch — a Ready frame is never produced-then-dropped; and (2) the only .await in next is the inner framed read, with dec synchronous after it, so a drop can only land before the counter advances. If either ever changed — a tokio upgrade that polls-and-collects, or an edit inserting an .await between decode and dec, or one that reconstructs the Framed per read — a decoded frame could be consumed from the buffer, dropped, and the counter left desynced, permanently breaking the recv direction (every later frame decrypts under the wrong nonce → Poly1305 fail). The fork MUST make this structural: fold decryption into a Decoder (a SecretboxCodec wrapping the length codec whose decode() authenticates and advances read_seq inside decode), so the recv counter lives in the Framed-owned codec and the decrypt is part of the value the chosen branch atomically receives — inheriting tokio-util's documented StreamExt::next cancel-safety verbatim. As a normative invariant regardless of implementation: no .await may sit between a frame leaving the read buffer and the secretbox open that advances read_seq; the counter advances only on a frame actually delivered to the caller; the Framed (with its partial-frame buffer) is retained across reads, never reconstructed. A regression test MUST drive next() under a select! with a competing always-ready branch and assert read_seq is unchanged when the read branch is not taken.

20.3 Stream framing & the unkeyed→keyed boundary

R-T6MUST The unkeyed→keyed boundary is closed by authentication (R-T7), never by a framing-buffer assertion
This is where it would be tempting to misuse TCP's stream nature, so the requirement is stated as a prohibition. A single Framed<DynTcpStream, BytesCodec> per connection is reused in place across the keying boundary: set_session_keys (tcp.rs) swaps only the cipher, leaving the Framed's persistent read buffer intact. TCP is a byte stream with no message boundaries, so a peer may legitimately coalesce or pipeline bytes after its last handshake frame (the length-prefixed codec reassembles complete frames regardless of segmentation); those trailing bytes would then be decoded as the first post-key frame. The naïve "fix" — assert the read buffer is empty at the boundary and abort if not — is WRONG and is forbidden: it treats a stream as if it had message boundaries, it forbids a peer from coalescing/pipelining (a legal TCP behaviour), and it buys nothing, because the boundary is already closed by authentication. R-T7 is the load-bearing closure: every byte that crosses into the keyed phase is AEAD-authenticated, so a residual byte from a peer that does not know the password cannot be a valid ciphertext and fails closed (and on the responder, reaching the final step already required the password); a residual byte from a peer that does know it is simply an authenticated, validly-sealed first application frame (a pipelined message), which is not an attack. Either way there is no "unkeyed byte interpreted unprotected." The correct, TCP-idiomatic guarantee is therefore authenticate every keyed byte (R-T7), which holds for any segmentation, coalescing, or pipelining — not a buffer-state assertion. The fork MUST NOT add a framing-buffer-empty check at the keying transition, and MUST instead rely on R-T7's invariant that no frame is ever processed on a keyed stream without AEAD authentication. (Should the keyed first-frame handling ever be changed, the regression to prevent is an unauthenticated path — caught by R-T7's gate — not a non-empty buffer.)
R-T7MUST Every frame on a keyed stream is authenticated — remove the ≤1-byte decrypt bypass
StreamCipher::Dual::dec (and the legacy Encrypt::dec) in tcp.rs return Ok(()) for a frame of length ≤ 1 without decrypting or authenticating it, passing the raw bytes through to Message::parse_from_bytes. This is the single path by which bytes reach the application parser on a keyed stream without AEAD — an inherited single-key artifact for "tiny control frames" that has no purpose under the two-key Dual cipher (a genuine sealed frame is always ≥ MACBYTES=16, since seal appends a 16-byte tag even to a 0-byte plaintext). The exposure is low-severity (a 0-byte frame parses to an empty Message{union:None} = no-op; a 1-byte frame is an unparseable/empty message reaching only no-op code behind the authorized gate; and the bypass returns before read_seq is touched, so it does not desync the counter) — but it is a real break in "every byte on a keyed channel is authenticated," it is the worst-case carryover channel for R-T6 (the one residual-byte path that is not AEAD-rejected), and its safety rests on the unenforced precondition that no parser ever treats a 0/1-byte frame as meaningful. The fork MUST delete the len() <= 1 shortcut on the keyed (Dual) path so any frame < MACBYTES is a decryption failure (fail-closed), matching the stricter rule the codebase already applies to at-rest secretbox payloads; a keyed heartbeat, if ever needed, MUST be a normal sealed frame (a 0-byte plaintext seals to 16 bytes), never an unauthenticated short frame. Related cap-accounting (fold in): the session frame cap is enforced on the ciphertext (wire) length, so the usable plaintext maximum is cap − MACBYTES; the spec MUST either document the cap as a ciphertext ceiling and state the max plaintext a sender may emit (the largest real frame is the 128 KiB file block, far below 32 MiB) or set the wire cap to intended_max_plaintext + MACBYTES so the intended plaintext and its sealed form both pass.
R-T8MUST Exactly one writer per FramedStream — multi-producer output funnels through a channel
This invariant holds and MUST be kept structural so a refactor cannot silently break it. Two concurrent writers to one FramedStream would byte-interleave their encoded frames on the wire → permanent framing desync (and, on the keyed stream, garbage ciphertext that fails every subsequent tag). The fork's many producers (video/audio/clipboard/camera/CM) correctly hold clones of an mpsc sender and the single run-loop task is the sole stream writer; the stream is never .split() for concurrent read/write on separate tasks, never wrapped in Arc<Mutex> for writing, never cloned. The fork MUST keep this an invariant: each FramedStream has exactly one writer; all multi-producer traffic fans in through an mpsc drained by the single owning task (the writer task of R-T3). This MUST be documented at the FramedStream type and the Connection.stream field, and SHOULD be enforced structurally (the write API stays &mut self; no API returns a second writable handle) so a future second-writer is a compile-visible error, not a silent wire corruption. (The codec/length-framing itself — partial frames, coalesced frames, byte-at-a-time delivery, a header split across reads, the variable-length prefix at every boundary, the prealloc cap decoupled from the declared length — is correct against arbitrary TCP segmentation by construction; the only obligation is to codify the Decoder contract it meets so a refactor cannot regress it.)
R-T16MUST Explicit length-delimited framing, and a strict reassemble → authenticate → parse order — never parse a TCP segment
TCP is a byte stream with no message boundaries, so the fork MUST impose its own framing and MUST NOT rely on a single read returning a whole message. The transport frames every message with an explicit self-describing length prefix — a variable 1–4 byte header whose low 2 bits select the header width and whose remaining 30 bits carry the payload length (bytes_codec.rs) — and decodes it with a stateful Decoder (a Head/Data(n) machine persisted across calls) driven by tokio-util's Framed loop: a partial frame returns Ok(None) and is buffered until complete; multiple coalesced frames are each emitted; a frame split across arbitrarily many segments — even one byte at a time, even with the header itself split across reads — is reassembled correctly. Message delimitation is therefore a property of the framing, never of TCP's segmentation or any "accidental packetization." The processing order is normative and MUST be exactly: (1) reassemble — the codec yields exactly ONE complete frame; (2) authenticate+decrypt — the AEAD (R-T7) opens the WHOLE frame; (3) parse — the protobuf decoder sees ONLY the decrypted, authenticated plaintext. No validation, decryption, or application parse may ever run on a partial frame or a raw TCP segment: FramedStream::next awaits the codec to a complete frame before calling key.dec, and the Message parse (connection.rs) runs only on the decrypted result. Consequently the Poly1305 tag covers exactly one complete ciphertext; a truncated, mis-framed, or coalesced ciphertext fails the tag fail-closed (never silently mis-parsed); and the protobuf parser is never exposed to attacker-chosen partial input. The frame-length cap (max_packet_length, R-S7: 4 KiB pre-key → 32 MiB post-key) and the speculative-allocation cap (MAX_PREALLOCATED_PAYLOAD_LEN = 256 KiB, decoupled from the declared length) are enforced at the framing layer, before reassembly completes, so an attacker-advertised huge length is rejected — and its allocation bounded — before any payload is buffered. The fork MUST preserve this framing and this strict order, and MUST codify the Decoder contract (R-T8) so a refactor cannot regress to parsing raw stream bytes.

20.4 Connection lifecycle & shutdown

R-T9MUST Graceful shutdown on SIGTERM — stop accepting, drain with a bounded deadline, then exit
The inherited service had no signal handling and relied entirely on ExecStop=pkill -f "rustdesk --" (SIGTERM → SIGKILL after TimeoutStopSec=30) with KillMode=mixed. In that shape, a systemctl restart (an upgrade) during an active session kills connections mid-write: a file block being written to disk on the peer is truncated, the framed write buffer is not flushed, and the per-connection on_close cleanup (CM notify, capture release, resolution/wallpaper restore) never runs. The fork MUST install a SIGTERM (and SIGINT) handler that (1) stops accepting — sets a shutdown flag and drops the TcpListener so new SYNs get RST; (2) drains — signals every live connection (via the AUTHED_CONNS/ALIVE_CONNS senders or a broadcast CancellationToken) to send its CloseReason, flush the writer, and deliver the CM Close, waiting up to a bounded drain deadline that MUST be shorter than TimeoutStopSec (e.g. ≤ 10 s); (3) force-aborts any still-live connection past the deadline and exits 0. The unit cgroup's TimeoutStopSec/SIGKILL path remains the backstop for a hung process — the requirement is that a clean drain path exists, so an upgrade does not corrupt an in-flight transfer, not that the backstop is removed.
R-T10MUST TCP keepalive on every accepted socket — the kernel backstop the NAT'd-client reality demands
No TCP keepalive is set anywhere (zero SO_KEEPALIVE in the tree); accepted sockets get only set_nodelay(true). The OS therefore does no half-open probing, and the sole liveness mechanism is the application's 30 s idle deadline (test_delay_timer, connection.rs) — which holds only because the box actively exchanges TestDelay keepalives. Because UDP is off precisely because the client is behind NAT (R-S13(d)), idle/rebinding/sleeping NAT mappings that vanish without a FIN/RST are the common case, not the rare one — every such dead peer holds an fd + task + capture subscription + CM IPC for up to ~31 s, and any future read path that fails to arm the app timer would hang forever. The fork MUST enable SO_KEEPALIVE on every accepted stream (via socket2::TcpKeepalive, already in the tree) as a kernel-level backstop to the app deadline, set immediately after set_nodelay. OS-aware (the knobs differ — this is normative): with_time(~30 s) maps to Linux/Android TCP_KEEPIDLE, macOS TCP_KEEPALIVE, Windows tcp_keepalive.keepalivetime; with_interval(~10 s) maps to TCP_KEEPINTVL / the Windows interval; with_retries(3) maps to TCP_KEEPCNT on Linux/Android/macOS but is compiled out on Windows (SIO_KEEPALIVE_VALS has no retry field — the count is OS-chosen), so the build MUST NOT assume a fixed probe count on Windows. The application 30 s deadline remains the portable primary guarantee; keepalive is the kernel backstop for any path that does not arm it.

20.5 OS-dependent socket configuration & semantics

R-T11MUST Listener socket options are OS-aware — no SO_REUSEPORT on the public listener; no hijackable SO_REUSEADDR on Windows
new_socket(reuse=true) (tcp.rs), used by the public listen_any_v4 listener, sets both SO_REUSEPORT and SO_REUSEADDR on Linux. SO_REUSEPORT lets another same-uid (root) process bind the same 0.0.0.0:21118 and join the kernel load-balance group, silently stealing a fraction of inbound connections — a local connection-hijack / selective-MITM primitive, and one that R-A4 cannot detect because its /proc/self/net self-check sees only the own process; a second listener in another process is invisible, directly violating R-D3's "no second listener of any kind." REUSEPORT is not needed by a single-instance systemd service. On Windows the danger is the twin: SO_REUSEADDR there means "steal the port" (mio refuses to set it on its own listeners for exactly this reason), so a Windows controlled box (R-D7b) is bind-hijackable. The fork MUST bind the public listener without SO_REUSEPORT (keep SO_REUSEADDR on Unix for clean TIME_WAIT restart only) and on Windows MUST either omit SO_REUSEADDR or set SO_EXCLUSIVEADDRUSE so the bind cannot be hijacked — restoring R-A4/R-D3's single-listener guarantee to an enforced property. Split new_socket's single reuse flag into reuse_addr/reuse_port (or add a listener-specific path) to express this. The v4-only bind (R-D5) is correct on every target and stays.
R-T12MUST Accept-loop observability — distinguish a timeout from an error, log fd-exhaustion, record shed events with source IP
An accept arm that collapses the benign poll-timeout and a real accept() error into one silent sleep (the inherited if let Ok(Ok((stream, addr))) = timeout(1000, l.accept()) { … } else { sleep(0.1) } at rendezvous_mediator.rs) is blind to fd-exhaustion: under the R-T1 flood accept() returns EMFILE/ENFILE, the box silently stops accepting with zero audit signal, the owner cannot distinguish "idle" from "drowning," and the kernel keeps signalling the socket readable so the loop busy-spins (worst on Android, the lowest fd ceiling). The fork MUST handle the three accept outcomes distinctly: a poll-timeout is normal (no log); a successful accept proceeds; a real accept() error MUST be observed (mapping EMFILE/ENFILE/WSAEMFILE/WSAENOBUFS via raw_os_error()) with an escalating bounded back-off that damps the busy-spin. This carries R-S10's observability obligation: the choke-point key-confirmation-failure and rate-limit-shed paths (server.rs) MUST emit the source IP and outcome (a bail with a static string that omits the IP leaves the "only audit signal on a serverless box," R-S10, and any fail2ban backstop impossible). So the fork MUST emit a structured record — source IP + outcome — for a key-confirmation failure, a rate-limit shed, an R-T1 pre-key drop-on-full, and an accept error. Crucially, per §20.0 rule 1, these records MUST be rate-limited or aggregated, never one log line per event: the shed, rate-limit, and accept-error paths are exactly the attacker-reachable hot paths of an in-progress flood, so a per-event log is itself a log/disk-amplification DoS. The required form is a lock-light per-category counter and a single periodic summary line (counts + most-recent source); the key-confirmation-failure record is already bounded per-source by the limiter (≤ the threshold per source per window) but the connection-flood-driven categories (shed, rate-limit, accept-error) MUST be aggregated. The result makes a flood visible to the operator without the observability itself becoming the second-order DoS.
R-T13SHOULD Android controlled-side networking lifecycle — network-change rebuild + Doze-survivable keepalive
The Android controlled server (the JNI startServer → start_direct_only/direct_server) runs in a foreground service but has no network-change or Doze handling. Android's radio sleep / Doze silently kills idle TCP NAT mappings within minutes, and a network switch (Wi-Fi↔cellular) kills the bound socket — with no SO_KEEPALIVE (R-T10) and only the 30 s app timer, a backgrounded Android controlled box can become silently unreachable, and the listener-rebuild path fires only on the disabled/port-change branch, not on silent socket death. The fork SHOULD register a ConnectivityManager.NetworkCallback (Kotlin) and, on onLost/onAvailable/network-switch, drive the existing listener-rebuild (listener = None) rather than letting it trigger only on config change; and SHOULD couple the R-T10 keepalive with the foreground-service partial wakelock so probes actually fire during radio sleep (without a wakelock Doze suspends the keepalive timer and the mapping dies anyway). Android-as-controlled is viewer-dominant in practice (R-D7a), so this is SHOULD, but it is the difference between a reliably-reachable and an intermittently-dead Android controlled box.
R-T14MUST Cross-backend cancellation-safety is a relied-upon, source-cited property — not folklore
It is widely and correctly held that cancellation and select/epoll behaviour is OS-dependent — but for the one operation it would most endanger here (a tokio TcpStream read/accept future dropped by select!/timeout), it does not produce a per-OS correctness difference, and the fork MUST record this guarantee with its source basis so it is relied upon deliberately, not assumed. By construction of the pinned async runtime (mio 1.0.3 / tokio 1.44.2): mio is edge-triggered on every real backend (epoll EPOLLET, kqueue EV_CLEAR, Windows IOCP+AFD emulated), but the actual byte transfer is a synchronous non-blocking std recv/accept inside do_io on all backends — no kernel-owned overlapped data buffer is ever in flight (the Windows AFD path carries only an AfdPollInfo handle+mask; there is no WSARecv in mio's TcpStream path), and dropping the read future only unlinks a readiness waiter. So dropping a read/accept consumes zero bytes and zero queued connections on epoll, kqueue, AND IOCP uniformly — which is exactly what makes R-T5's decrypt-in-place nonce scheme safe on every target OS, and the drop-heavy select! correct cross-platform. The fork MUST document this guarantee (with the mio/tokio citations) as the basis for R-T5, so a future contributor does not "fix" a non-problem or, worse, refactor in a way that introduces a real per-OS hazard (e.g. a hand-rolled WSARecv overlapped read). The per-OS differences that do bite are not the dropped-future cancellation — they are the socket-config/lifecycle items R-T10/R-T11/R-T13 (keepalive knobs, REUSEADDR-on-Windows, Android Doze), each handled explicitly there.

20.6 Transport-adjacent conformance — the obligations the keyed path depends on

R-T15MUST Satisfy the transport-adjacent obligations of R-P12/R-S2/R-S9/R-S10 the keyed path depends on
These obligations sharpen §10–§11 with the specific guarantees the keyed transport rests on; each MUST be satisfied: (a) R-P12 secret-zeroization is exhaustive — curve25519-dalek 4.1.3 implements the Zeroize trait but not Drop/ZeroizeOnDrop, so a secret that is not explicitly wiped lingers in freed memory on an attacker-inducible abort/timeout path. The implementation MUST explicitly zeroize every such secret on every exit path: the isk (the layer's master secret, from which mac_key and both directional keys derive) in both recv_step2_with/recv_step3; the initiator's ephemeral scalar ya on the decompress(Yb) error early-return (the responder's symmetric path wipes its scalar, and the initiator MUST match it); the session keys and yb_scalar held in InitiatorAwaitConfirm/ResponderAwaitConfirm, which MUST carry a Drop/#[zeroize(drop)] so they are wiped on the R-P14b step-timeout drop; and the in-memory g/K points (the draft flags the PRS-laden generator as the most side-channel-critical substep) — via Zeroizing wrappers and a manual/derived Drop on the two *AwaitConfirm structs (libs/pake/src/lib.rs). (b) the inherited LOGIN_FAILURES limiter (connection.rs) carries the exact unbounded-growth / never-decaying / full-IPv6-address anti-patterns R-S10 condemns; even where its bump paths are unreachable (headless pinned off, the terminal's second-OS-credential failure scope (TerminalOsLogin) excised, the unkeyed branch unreachable) it MUST be excised so the live limiter is unambiguously the bounded, decaying, per-v4-source GUESS_FAILURES in cpace.rs — a dead but anti-pattern-bearing structure on an exposed box is debt R-S10 forbids. (c) the legacy Hash{salt,challenge} emission in on_open (connection.rs) MUST be deleted, not merely demoted (R-S2): with the challenge/response collapsed into the PAKE its consumer is gone, so it is a redundant post-key emission, and R-S2 requires it "deleted, not demoted." These are conformance closures that keep §10–§11's guarantees structural, not new exposures.
§20 in one paragraph

The keyed channel's security against a public-internet TCP attacker MUST rest on construction, not luck: no keying bypass, no nonce reuse, no plaintext fallback, no cross-boundary infiltration, the bidirectional-key-reuse of Appendix C #1 closed by the per-direction keys of R-P2/R-P10. The requirements here are what make that hold and keep holding — the connection-flood bound that keeps an unauthenticated flood from OOM-ing the host (R-T1, the section's one availability-critical control), the cancellation-safety invariants that MUST be structural rather than emergent so a single careless edit cannot silently desync the stream cipher or admit an unauthenticated byte (R-T2–R-T8), the lifecycle/keepalive/OS-aware socket configuration and flood-safe observability (R-T9–R-T14), and the transport-adjacent conformance the keyed path depends on (R-T15) — every one of them held to the §20.0 discipline that a transport control must never become the next vulnerability. Implementing a TCP server correctly is the work; §20 names it so the fork does it deliberately, not naïvely.

Appendix B · Socket & port ledger — every bind site accounted for

The §1 promise — understand every open port — made auditable. Every socket the binary can bind on the Linux controlled-service path, its original (upstream) purpose, and the exact mechanism that keeps it off the hardened box. Verified by a whole-binary sweep (the application crate, hbb_common, the 38 git-sourced crate records, and the native C codecs), cross-checked against the compiled feature set; the runtime invariant is asserted by R-A4 and the build-time absence by R-A6. Net reachable surface of the shipped binary run as --server: one v4 TCP listener on 21118, zero UDP, plus local AF_UNIX IPC.

Verification basis. A static whole-binary sweep finds no listener in any in-tree libs/, git-dep fork, or native lib beyond the rows above; the compiled-feature proof shows webrtc/plugin_framework are not in the Debian feature set (so those paths are absent, not merely call-dead); R-A4 enumerates /proc/self/net/{tcp,udp,udp6} at startup and asserts exactly one TCP listener and zero UDP sockets of any kind on the running --server process (R-R2b) — KCP and the UDP hole-punch are compiled out in both roles (TCP-only, R-S13/R-D4), so the --server binds no UDP at all and a box that is also viewing connects out over TCP (an outbound connect is no listener and binds no UDP); and the R-A6 call-graph guard proves the mediator/probe paths (including the IPC CheckTestNatType Drop reach, R-S11) stay unreachable from the service entry. On a built binary the surface is confirmed with ss -lntup (one TCP :21118, no UDP listener), lsof -nP -p <pid> -iU (the AF_UNIX channels), and strace -f -e trace=bind,listen,connect (a single bind/listen, no outbound connect).

Appendix C · Verified vulnerability inventory — severity & disposition

The security findings behind §2's failure modes, ranked by severity and each mapped to the requirement that disposes of it and how: replace (design superseded by the PAKE), excise (compiled out), fix (logic changed), harden (bounded / permissioned), accept (a deliberate §2 scope decision), or remediate (outstanding work). All were verified by direct read of the pinned tree (analysis, not demonstrated exploits) and are cross-referenced, in the note immediately below, to the public RustDesk Common Vulnerabilities and Exposures record that independently corroborates most of them — and conspicuously omits the most serious. "Tier 1" = a flaw that on its own justifies not exposing upstream RustDesk directly.

Provenance & the public CVE record — what is corroborated, and the one thing that is not

Every row below was reached independently, by direct read of the pinned RustDesk 1.4.7 tree. After that audit, an independent security researcher's coordinated disclosure — dated 5 March 2026, accompanied by a published proof-of-concept dossier and a corroborating Western Australian Government cyber-security advisory (reference 20260309001, 9 March 2026) — produced a batch of Common Vulnerabilities and Exposures identifiers: CVE-2026-30783 through CVE-2026-30785 and CVE-2026-30789 through CVE-2026-30798 against the RustDesk client and the open-source RustDesk Server, CVE-2026-3598 against RustDesk Server Pro, and the Microsoft Windows file-transfer issue CVE-2026-2490. That batch independently confirms most of this inventory — the at-rest configuration cipher (#14 ↔ CVE-2026-30785, CVE-2026-30791), the trust-anchor and configuration re-homing (#6 ↔ CVE-2026-30791, CVE-2026-30797, plus the unauthenticated strategy-merge twin CVE-2026-30792, CVE-2026-30798), the unstretched password proof and its rate limiter (#7 and #21 ↔ CVE-2026-30789, CVE-2026-30790), and the symbolic-link time-of-check-to-time-of-use defect (#10 ↔ CVE-2026-2490). Two conclusions follow, and the reader is meant to draw both.

The pinned base is unrepaired. The RustDesk 1.4.7 tree this fork builds on was committed on 2 June 2026 — nearly three months after that disclosure — and still ships the vulnerable code for every client-side item in this table. Where upstream touched a finding at all, it did so by halves: the fixed all-zero nonce behind CVE-2026-30785 became a random per-record nonce in the symmetric_crypt routine of hbb_common's password_security module, yet the encryption key is still the raw machine UUID zero-padded to length with no key-derivation function (#14); and the invalid-certificate-accepting outbound path behind CVE-2026-30794 is now gated behind an allow-insecure-tls-fallback option that is off by default, so it is no longer a live exposure on a stock build and earns no row of its own. The only defensible reading is that upstream RustDesk does not treat the confidentiality and authentication of an internet-exposed session as a release-blocking concern — which is precisely the premise this fork exists to reverse.

The single most consequential finding is absent from that record. #1 — the live session transport reusing one libsodium secretbox key in both directions — is, to the limit of an exhaustive search of the CVE batch, the disclosure's own proof-of-concept dossier, RustDesk's SECURITY.md, and every upstream encryption discussion thread, unreported as of this writing. It must not be conflated with the widely-cited CVE-2026-30785. That identifier is the configuration file at rest — the password_security module, a literal all-zero nonce, a machine-UUID key — exploited by a local reader of the file. #1 is the live wire — the Encrypt type in the tcp module of hbb_common, sequence-counter nonces, one key in both directions — exploited by a passive network eavesdropper who never touches the host. They are different defects, in different code, with different victims. Any disclosure of #1 must make that distinction its first sentence, or be dismissed as a duplicate of CVE-2026-30785.

Finding #1 in full — key establishment, blast radius & proof of concept

Why one key serves both directions. The session key is generated once, by the connecting viewer. In create_symmetric_key_msg (the common module of rustdesk/rustdesk) the viewer calls secretbox::gen_key() a single time, seals that one key to the controlled side inside an ephemeral Curve25519 box (NaCl crypto_box — a fresh key-pair is generated per handshake, so that layer's own all-zero nonce is harmless), sends it as the PublicKey.symmetric_value field, and installs it locally through set_key. The controlled side recovers the identical key with Encrypt::decode and installs it through its own set_key. Both endpoints now hold a single Encrypt value wrapping that one key, and each seals its outbound frames and opens the peer's inbound frames under that same key with sequence-counter nonces that both start at zero. The two-time pad is therefore structural — not that the two directions might collide, but that frame N in each direction is guaranteed to.

Blast radius — this is not a direct-IP vulnerability. The defective code lives in hbb_common, vendored verbatim by both rustdesk/rustdesk (the client, pinned here at 1.4.7) and rustdesk/rustdesk-server (which builds both the rendezvous server hbbs and the relay server hbbr), and it is engaged on every transport that keys the secretbox: NAT-hole-punched TCP, intranet TCP, KCP-over-UDP, and relayed TCP — that is, the ordinary identifier-based connection that the overwhelming majority of RustDesk users make through a rendezvous server and, on relay fallback, a relay server. The serverless direct-IP listener is the one path this defect does not touch, precisely because it never keys the secretbox at all — it transmits in cleartext, the separate finding #2. The relayed case is the most exposed: hbbr is a blind byte-pipe carrying the end-to-end stream, so the relay operator — including whoever runs RustDesk's default public relay infrastructure — sees both reused-nonce ciphertext streams with no additional network position.

Proof of concept — passive capture suffices. Record one active session's frames in both directions (from a shared local network, any transit hop, or simply as the relay operator). For each sequence index N, exclusive-or the controlled-side ciphertext with the viewer-side ciphertext: the XSalsa20 keystream cancels, leaving the exclusive-or of the two plaintexts. Seed crib-dragging with the fixed leading protobuf tag bytes that prefix nearly every frame (for instance the video_frame and key_event field numbers) and with structurally predictable content such as video key-frame headers and cursor coordinates; recovering either direction's frame at index N immediately yields the other. No key recovery and no man-in-the-middle are required for disclosure. For an active attacker the reused Poly1305 one-time key additionally admits frame forgery — injected input or spoofed content that authenticates correctly.

The remedy is upstream's, and it is generic. Independently of this fork's PAKE, RustDesk must derive two distinct directional keys from the established session key — for instance HKDF with separate info labels for the controlled-to-viewer and viewer-to-controlled directions — or, at minimum, fold a direction byte into the nonce so the two directions can never share a (key, nonce). The change is confined to the Encrypt type in hbb_common and the handshake's set_key call sites, which would pass an initiator/responder role; it is wire-compatible and repairs every server-mediated connection at once. This fork applies the same correction as a by-product of R-P2/R-P10 (per-direction keys derived from the PAKE secret), but the defect and its fix belong to upstream and its entire hbbs/hbbr user base — not to the serverless direct-IP build.

#Inherited vulnerability (upstream 1.4.7)Disposition & class
Tier 1 — serious; each alone cements "not safe to expose raw"
1Bidirectional key reuse on the live session transport — the Encrypt type in the tcp module of hbb_common (tcp.rs) installs one libsodium secretbox (XSalsa20-Poly1305) key for both directions and derives the 24-byte nonce from a per-direction sequence counter that begins at zero on each side with no direction tag, so the controlled side's N-th frame and the viewer's N-th frame are sealed under an identical (key, nonce). This is a textbook two-time pad — the exclusive-or of two same-index ciphertexts cancels the XSalsa20 keystream — and RustDesk's fixed protobuf field numbers (for instance video_frame = 6, key_event = 15) supply abundant known-plaintext cribs; the reused Poly1305 one-time authenticator additionally permits frame forgery, not merely disclosure. It defeats every "encrypted" transport (punch-hole, intranet, KCP-over-UDP, and relay), and because the open-source RustDesk relay server (hbbr) is a blind byte-pipe carrying the end-to-end stream, the relay operator alone — including any operator of public relay infrastructure — observes both reused-nonce streams. This is the most serious inherited finding, and it is uniquely not covered by any published CVE: it is distinct from the at-rest CVE-2026-30785 (see the note above). Severity is High; it is a defect of the server-mediated encrypted transports — not of the direct-IP path, whose separate plaintext exposure is #2REPLACE + FIX per-direction keys derived from the PAKE ISK by HKDF (R-P2/R-P10); two distinct directional keys asserted at runtime and proven by known-answer test (R-A5/R-A10)
2Direct-IP mode is plaintext — in both roles — challenge / salted-hash / input / screen / files in cleartext; hash offline-crackable (§2 failure A). The connecting viewer is equally affected: Client::_start returns an unkeyed stream for a bare IP/domain:port (client.rs) before secure_connection (§4.4)REPLACE mandatory PAKE keys every transport before any app message, on the responder and the initiator; the secure flag and both unkeyed paths are deleted (R-S1, R-S13, R-S6, §10), so the initiator is keyed wherever a box views
2bNative-decode RCE surface — every full viewer — any box, when it views a hostile-but-password-correct peer, feeds attacker bytes to in-process libvpx/libyuv/opus/zstd + (Windows-only) CLIPRDR C (§4.4); the PAKE authenticates the peer but cannot make a hostile box safe. Every box is a full viewer (one binary, R-R2b), so this is universal — present on every build, reached whenever a box views. AV1/libaom is handled separately by the runtime quarantine and dependency removal in #87/#88.ACCEPT + SHOULD-sandbox a universal residual: the remaining decoders ship in every binary (no build omits the viewer), reached only when a box views a peer it deliberately connected to; bounded operationally (connect only to peers you trust) + deep-link confirmation/channel restriction (R-S13/R-X6); R-B5a pins the codecs; recorded as a documented residual not closable by keying — the fork SHOULD sandbox the decode path. Pinned codecs ≠ CVE-free (vcpkg C libs are outside cargo-audit — §13, Appendix D)
3Fetch-and-run updater — executes a server-supplied URL with no signature/hash check, privileged (updater.rs)EXCISE remove the module + --update + the Flutter keys (R-X1); CI-grep absent (R-A6); no egress (R-D6)
4set_raw() plaintext tunnel — port-forward/RDP drops the cipher after login (connection.rs, port_forward.rs)FIX remove the key-strip so tunnelled bytes stay in the secretbox (R-S5); assert (R-A3); wire-capture test (R-A9)
5Unbounded pre-auth frame DoS — set_max_packet_length never called; ~1 GiB/connection amplification (bytes_codec.rs)FIX set the cap (≤ 4 KiB pre-PAKE, ≈ 32 MiB after) (R-S7); assert it is set (R-A5)
6Trust-anchor / server re-homing — unsigned-JSON config string, renamed exe, ungated --import-config, ConfigureUpdate + its TestNatResponse twin (common.rs), rustdesk://config + D-Bus NewConnectionEXCISE + FIX bake/pin the anchor, get_key() ignores overrides, drop custom_server/--config/--import-config/deep-link writes (R-X3/X4/X6); startup-assert anchor == pinned (R-A4); mediator out kills the network reach (R-D4)
Tier 2 — real, but conditional / lower-severity / post-auth
7Rate-limiter DoS — unbounded growth (IPv6 /64 memory-exhaustion), never-decaying permanent lockout (connection.rs)FIX bound + decay + per-source + log (R-S10); owner-safety test (R-A8); v4-only retires the IPv6 vector (R-D5)

(Full-filesystem reach itself is intended, not a defect.)FIX no-follow opens (openat+O_NOFOLLOW) close the TOCTOU, asserted per write (R-S8/R-A5); full-FS reach is a documented single-mode capability per §2 — no confinement toggle (a toggle would violate R-S12)
11Terminal ⇒ root PTY, no Linux re-auth, enable-terminal default-on (terminal_service.rs)ACCEPTED full control for the authenticated owner is the intended single mode on the sovereign shared-password box — pin enable-terminal = Y at the config funnel (immutable: is_option_can_save rejects every runtime write and the SwitchPermission widener is removed, R-X8/R-S16); the second-credential PAM/LogonUserW path is excised (R-X14/R-S18)
12Source-IP whitelist (check_whitelist) — an IP ACL that only ever gates an already-keyed peer (post-CPace, connection.rs), so it can never reduce attack surface, only deny the authenticated ownerEXCISE check_whitelist + the whitelist option; CPace is the sole gate (R-S9/R-D2)
13LAN broadcast leak — MAC / ID / hostname / active-user / platform to any pinger (lan.rs)EXCISE the UDP listener (R-X5); assert no UDP listener (R-A4); CI-grep (R-A6)
14At-rest config "encryption" is machine-UUID obfuscation, not secrecy — the symmetric_crypt routine in hbb_common's password_security module keys the libsodium secretbox with the raw machine UUID zero-padded to length, with no key-derivation function (CVE-2026-30785). RustDesk 1.4.7 partially remediated the disclosed defect — the formerly fixed all-zero nonce is now a random per-record nonce — but the machine-UUID key remains, so a local reader of the file who also knows the host's machine UUID can recover the stored credential. In the fork that stored credential is not the plaintext password: it is the Argon2id PRS (R-P1) — a memory-hard salted hash — so a config read yields the hash, not the password, and the recovered value is therefore not the user's password for reuse elsewhere and not the OS/sudo credential even when those are reused. The narrower residual is that the PRS is connect-equivalent (a balanced PAKE keys a session directly from it), so a cold read on the endpoint can still impersonate the viewer to that boxHARDEN + ACCEPT filesystem-permission the config (R-S9) — now a memory-hard salted hash at rest, comparable to SSH's, not a recoverable plaintext; the residual is the connect-equivalent cold-read on the endpoint, inherent to a balanced PAKE and out of scope (§2). A TPM-seal of the at-rest wrapper, or not host-bind-mounting the config, further reduces even that cold-read
15IPC: Windows pipe allow_everyone_create (same-session) + legacy main channel generic config/proxy write shapes (ipc.rs)FIX same-user DACL + per-arm write allowlist (R-S11) as the transport/config-write prerequisite; Windows pipe creation now uses explicit SDDL DACLs, non-generic client open rights that omit create-instance, and server PID/executable verification; installed-service credential/action authority is the stricter #25–#29 class, not closed by this row alone
16Dependency advisory gate — Rust, Dart/Pub, and vcpkg native-codec advisory coverage must be repeatable and fail-closed, with every remaining advisory either fixed, proven unreachable for a shipped role, or explicitly accepted with a reasonGATED R-R3/R-A7: scripts/audit.sh runs pinned cargo-audit + cargo-deny check advisories; scripts/dart-audit.sh runs offline OSV for Pub; scripts/native-codec-watch.sh keeps the vcpkg C/C++ watch ledger synchronized with the requirements and source pins
17Tier-1-class (host expansion): os_login → PAM desktop-session-start — a peer's LoginRequest.os_login drives root pam::authenticate()/open_session() + startwm.sh-sources-dotfiles-as-root (linux_desktop_manager.rs); pre-auth on upstream's plaintext direct path when allow-linux-headless is enabled (a default-off toggle the §17 box does not need — capture is independent) = a remote, rate-limited (per-IP failure counter) root-context PAM oracleEXCISE compile out the PAM/session-spawn path on every build (a second OS credential, R-X14/R-S18), keep capture discovery, pin allow-linux-headless off; CI-grep absent (R-A6)
182FA Telegram egress — hardcoded api.telegram.org POST (auth_2fa.rs), gated on bot/2fa not api-server, so R-D6's api-server-empty pin does not silence itPIN/EXCISE hard-pin bot/2fa empty or excise send_2fa_code_to_telegram (R-D6); egress-grep (R-A6)
19Viewer-role: a keyed hostile peer writes attacker-controlled data into the viewer's persistent PeerConfig via in-session Misc::BackNotification/PeerInfo/TerminalResponse arms (io_loop.rs, client.rs), including the former peer-metadata selection of persisted keyboard_mode — survives R-S13 keying; the initiator twin of R-S11 (IPC-scoped)GATE validate/bound the allowed writes, reject unsupported privacy implementations, keep peer-derived keyboard-mode compatibility runtime-only, and extend the R-S11 config-write allowlist to the client dispatch (R-S15); AST-assert (R-A6)
20Post-key decompression amplification — receive-path zstd::decode_all is output-unbounded (compress.rs); the R-S7 frame cap bounds input, not inflation (either role)FIX cap decompressed output (set_max_output_size/streaming) on every receive decompress (R-S7)
21Password-proof capture & credential bypass — the login proof is an unstretched SHA-256(SHA-256(password ‖ salt) ‖ challenge), verified by verify_h1 in the connection handler (CVE-2026-30789, CVE-2026-30790). Simple cross-connection replay is in fact blocked upstream, because the challenge is a fresh six-character value drawn from the cryptographically-secure get_auto_password generator on every connection (a property the fork discards along with the whole challenge/response, R-S2 — its replay defense is instead the per-connection fresh sid + ephemeral scalar, R-P14c); the real exposure is twofold. (a) On the cleartext direct path (#2) — or on any "encrypted" session laid bare by #1 — the captured salt, challenge and proof are offline-recoverable on commodity hardware, because the hash is fast and unstretched (no Argon2, scrypt, or PBKDF2). (b) The SwitchSidesResponse handler authorizes a session with no password check at all upon a matching role-switch identifier inside a ten-second window, and the resulting from_switch state also bypasses two-factor authentication; the thirty-second is_recent_session re-admission likewise skips two-factor authentication. Both are narrow but genuine password-less authorizationsFIX the PAKE removes the offline-crackable proof from the security boundary entirely (R-S6); the switch-sides and recent-session grants are re-gated behind a confirmed PAKE-keyed stream with the re-admission window set to zero (R-S2, R-X5), and the two-factor bypass closes with them
22Viewer auto-leaks stored OS credentials & device fingerprint — the connecting viewer persists os-username/os-password per peer and auto-sends them on every connect as LoginRequest.os_login (client.rs: os_*=get_option("os-username"/"os-password")), plus the stable hwid fingerprint when trust-this-device. A malicious or substituted peer (a different box answering at the same address) thus harvests the operator's OS credentials with no further interaction — a client-side credential leak the server-only audit cannot see, surfaced by the §4.4 initiator sweepEXCISE (symmetric) delete the client os_login/hwid senders, the persisted os-username/os-password options, and the OSLogin/hwid proto fields (R-S18) — with the senders gone there is nothing to harvest regardless of substitution; CI-greps absent on both sides (R-A6)
Post-audit external disclosures — the "Exploitarium" public zero-day dump (June 2026, pseudonymous researcher "bikini"); the two RustDesk items are both closed on the fork (#23 dead by construction, #24 confined by an added fix)
23Relay-session security downgrade (Exploitarium, June 2026; researcher-classified "Critical-class", no CVE assigned; independently analyzed in upstream RustDesk discussion #15492) — a malicious relay/rendezvous strips the signed peer key so a session expected to be secure continues on the secure=false path with no peer-key agreement, then observes and injects plaintext control messages after the legitimate login authorizes (client.rs empty / public-key-empty handshake fallbacks; rendezvous_mediator.rs relay-secure flag; server.rs secure-conditional keying with key-confirmation cleared on the non-secure path). A concrete PoC of the attacker-controlled rr.secure / plaintext-downgrade surface this spec already identified (§4.1 row 4; R-S1; the same class as #1–#2)REPLACE already neutralized by construction: the mandatory CPace PAKE runs unconditionally at the create_tcp_connection choke point with the secure parameter deleted (R-S1/R-P14) — there is no non-secure path to fail open into — and the rendezvous mediator + relay are compiled out (R-D4/R-SV4), so the required attacker position (control of the relay/rendezvous metadata path) does not exist. Verified post-disclosure: rendezvous_mediator.rs absent; create_tcp_connection keys every connection unconditionally
24FileTransfer authorization scope bypass (CVE-2026-58056, CWE-863, CVSS 7.6; Exploitarium, June 2026) — RustDesk gates post-auth input / screenshot / display-capture on per-capability flags and broad self.authorized state rather than the session's AuthConnType, and the FileTransfer post-login branch (unlike terminal/view-camera) does not clear keyboard/input, so a peer authorized only for FileTransfer can inject mouse/keyboard input and reach the display-capture handlers (connection.rs). The vulnerable shape was inherited by the fork (the FileTransfer branch did not clear self.keyboard; the input/capture handlers gated on the capability flag + view-camera/port-forward early-returns, never on AuthConnType::Remote) — now closed (see disposition)FIX (defense-in-depth) closed even though it was moot under §2 (reaching a FileTransfer session requires the full permanent password = the trusted owner, who already has full control — terminal-root, full-filesystem, remote, R-S8/R-X8 — and no less-trusted FileTransfer grant exists: approve-mode + full access pinned, no rendezvous/broker, R-S16/R-D4), because the fork already confines view-camera/terminal/port-forward from input so FileTransfer was the lone un-confined type (least-privilege coherence). Two-part fix in connection.rs, both regression-gated in verify.sh: (1) an AuthConnType allowlist in on_message — INPUT (mouse/key/pointer) is Remote-only, DISPLAY capture/control (screenshot, capture/switch-display, resolution/refresh, privacy, restart) is Remote-or-ViewCamera; screen capture has no capability flag, so this guard is load-bearing for it; (2) the FileTransfer login branch now clears the keyboard/block_input/privacy_mode capability flags (mirroring the terminal/view-camera branches), closing the flag-gated sinks — input, the block_input local-console DoS, and the <1.2.4 turn_off_privacy compat. A FileTransfer peer now reaches neither input nor screen capture. The general class this instance belongs to — every peer-triggerable capability keying on the session's AuthConnType, never a decoupled per-capability flag — is now pinned as the normative R-S19 (§7); the full structural closure of that class (deriving all capability booleans from AuthConnType before any login-option is applied, and keying the remaining flag-gated sinks — host clipboard-text write, peer→host audio, block_input, privacy, restart — on the session type too) is tracked there
Service-owned local privilege-boundary findings — IPC must not let an unprivileged local process mint remote credentials, rewrite service policy, or drive privileged service actions
25Installed-service unattended password write over ordinary IPC — in service mode the permanent password/PRS is honored by the privileged unattended host, but the inherited IPC shape treats permanent-password as a normal config key. A same-session/same-user process that can reach the main IPC can therefore set the credential that the root/SYSTEM/service-owned host will later accept remotely. This is local-to-remote privilege escalation in the product's own security model: the local caller may not gain a root shell immediately, but it mints a remote-access secret for a privileged or service-owned unattended host.FIX R-S11b-2a/R-S11b-2b/R-S11b-3c close the ordinary IPC write, whole-config sync, standalone salt read, password-storage/salt sync, and generic config write paths for service-marked --server processes while preserving user-owned writes for user-owned servers. FIX Linux: R-S11b-2c exposes only a typed RequestServiceOwnedUnattendedPasswordChange service operation, authorizes it with polkit auth_admin using a SO_PEERCRED-derived pid,start-time,uid subject, then accepts the final CommitServiceOwnedUnattendedPasswordChange only from the root service into a service-owned receiver. FIX Windows: R-S11b-2d exposes the same typed service request shape, exposes it to the UI/CLI only from an already elevated RustDesk process, proves the connected _service caller by named-pipe client-token impersonation before forwarding, and accepts the final commit only from a LocalSystem service peer into a service-owned receiver. FIX macOS: R-S11b-2e/R-S11c-1f exposes a typed RequestMacosServiceOwnedUnattendedPasswordChange { password, authorization } _service request. The UI first connects through the authenticated trusted-helper client path, obtains an external form for the explicit non-shared timeout-zero RustDesk Authorization Services right, and only then sends the proposed password and authorization in that single request. The LaunchDaemon creates/updates that right as admin-only, non-shared, and timeout-zero, verifies the external form noninteractively, enforces the password-size bound, and writes the authorized value directly into the root LaunchDaemon credential store; no request-id state machine or pending plaintext secret cache exists. The old macOS final CommitServiceOwnedUnattendedPasswordChange main-IPC commit is rejected. The service-owned LaunchAgent obtains the root credential only through a typed MacosServiceOwnedPermanentPasswordSnapshotRequest; the LaunchDaemon answers only when the service IPC peer is the installed app/trusted-helper pair, the peer is a live --server --service-owned-server process, launchctl print gui/<uid>/<label> reports the requesting pid as the expected /Library/LaunchAgents/..._server.plist job, and the parsed LaunchAgent plist file and parent are root:wheel, non-symlink, non-group/world-writable, ACL-free, with Label, ProgramArguments, RunAtLoad, and KeepAlive exactly matching the service-owned server job. The snapshot carries local root password storage/salt, never a preset fallback, and the LaunchAgent applies it as a runtime-only PRS overlay that is not serialized into user config.
26Linux/macOS _service whole-config sync crossed the user↔root boundary — Data::SyncConfig returned or replaced whole Config/Config2 between the active user and root/LaunchDaemon service. That carried connect-equivalent credential material, identity/salt/key material, trust state, reachability policy, and hardening-relevant options. Filesystem permissions did not cure a root service handing the material to a user process, and an allowlist on individual option writes did not cure a whole-struct import.FIX per R-S11/R-S11b/R-S11c: whole-config service sync is deleted; _service admits only narrow typed messages (Data::Test liveness, Linux's R-S11b-2c service-owned unattended-password request, and macOS's R-S11b-2e authorized password request plus launchd-owned runtime-snapshot service-owned unattended-password exchange); stale-socket probes no longer read config; the Data::SyncConfig(Some(_)) receiver write arm is absent; verify.sh and apple-conform-check.sh assert the bus is not reintroduced.
27Windows _service trusts local callers for privileged target/action selection — the service-side session switch accepts caller-supplied target session data, while the legitimate remote path validates policy only before sending the IPC message. The privileged receiver must not rely on the unprivileged sender having checked share_rdp, target existence, current session, or remote authorization. The same channel also exposes privileged SAS/HKLM-touching behavior as a generic service command.FIX per R-S11c/R-S19: the raw service commands are removed. Data::UserSid, Data::SAS, and the client helper that sent caller-chosen sessions no longer exist; the Windows service loop no longer dispatches session-switch or SAS/HKLM actions from local IPC; selected-session and physical-console SAS requests fail closed. Windows service-owned share_rdp policy is now a typed RequestServiceOwnedShareRdp service action: the main IPC rejects it, the UI/FFI path sends it to _service, the service validates an elevated pipe-client token and commits HKLM directly as LocalSystem, and the old reg add ... share_rdp UI-side writer is absent. Future service-mediated session switching or SAS must be reintroduced only as a typed receiver-authorized capability tied to an authenticated Remote session and current policy state.
28Helper IPC ambient trust — CM/audio/whiteboard-style local helpers are privilege-separation mechanisms, not trusted internal buses. In particular, pre-login CM file operations make local file read/write/delete/rename reachable before connection ownership is proven; Linux _pa previously let any same-UID client name a PulseAudio source and receive raw host audio; same-UID socket ownership is not a proof that this specific RustDesk connection owns the helper; and fixed helper endpoints such as _cm must not disclose per-connection authority to an unproven local listener.FIX R-S11c-4a/R-S11c-4b closes the CM file-authority instance: each authenticated server Connection mints a random CM token and records it with the active AuthConnType plus server-side file capability; CM validates (conn_id, AuthConnType, token) against the main server before add_connection, so forged/stale/wrong-token local Data::Login cannot self-mint authority. Desktop file work then requires Data::AuthorizedFS carrying the same token on that validated stream, while legacy desktop Data::FS is reject-only before raw WriteBlock or handle_fs. File authority is Remote/FileTransfer-only and server-file-capability-bound; Android remains in-process but uses the same receiver-side authority derivation before handle_fs. FIX R-S11c-7 closes the direct Linux _pa capture surface and its Linux _cm-poisoning dependency: audio capture starts only after an owner-identity/token lease minted from the active audio subscriber set, authenticated live _cm/_pa process-identity binding (pid, uid, Linux proc start time, current executable, expected CM mode, server-scoped CM launch token, server-parent ancestry), a pre-send endpoint identity check, and helper-side validation against the token-owning process through the owner UID's main IPC after authenticating that endpoint against the serialized owner identity; missing, wrong, stale, wrong-peer, launch-tokenless, non-descendant, ambient-validator, or unauthenticated fixed-path listener cases fail before source resolution or PulseAudio open. FIX R-S11c-11 closes the remaining fixed-path _cm endpoint-selection class before cm_auth_token, file authority, chat, voice-call state, or future helper leases are disclosed: macOS and Windows require process-shape validation plus mutual launch-token HMAC proof (Windows also uses authenticated _cm connects for the secondary clipboard/privacy server clients), and Linux keeps live process-identity validation plus the same pre-disclosure proof. Stale, preexisting, launch-tokenless, wrong-mode, wrong-token, fixed-path squatting listeners, and same-binary server signing-oracle attempts fail before Data::Login or any per-connection CM token is sent. FIX R-S11c-22 closes the Windows CM non-file clipboard read gap: authenticated endpoint proof is no longer enough to call check_clipboard_cm(); the root clipboard service must carry a subscribed Remote connection's conn_id, AuthConnType, and cm_auth_token, and CM revalidates that live clipboard capability through the main server before reading the desktop clipboard. FIX R-S11c-12 closes the Windows terminal helper pipe-binding class: the service-created helper pipes are first-instance/local-only named pipes with the existing SYSTEM-plus-target-user DACL, and after ConnectNamedPipe the service queries GetNamedPipeClientProcessId and accepts only the PID returned by CreateProcessAsUserW; same-user pipe-name disclosure or first-client racing cannot select the helper endpoint.
29macOS privileged-service packaging hazards — a LaunchDaemon must not execute root code from an app bundle, a user-writable path, or any path whose authority is created by an admin script chowning active-user-controlled contents into root ownership. Predictable daemon logs in /tmp, shell-based daemon launch, dormant privileged update scripts that copy a caller-supplied source tree into the service root, active-user preference imports into root service state, ownership-only privileged-helper identity checks, path-only GUI peer trust, and reuse of a stale /Library/PrivilegedHelperTools helper are the same boundary failure: privileged service state must be laid down by the privileged installer flow as the exact signed helper chosen by the current app build and then only verified by the service-enabling script and service IPC receiver.FIX per R-S11c and the Apple source-conformance bar (R-R2): the LaunchDaemon uses direct ProgramArguments pointing at /Library/PrivilegedHelperTools/com.carriez.rustdesk_service, with a root-owned support-directory working directory; daemon stdout/stderr live under /Library/Logs/RustDesk, not /tmp; install.scpt creates the root-owned helper/log/support/root-preference directories and quoted launchd plists, verifies the bundled service helper is a non-symlink executable signed by the pinned Developer ID Team ID with the expected helper identifier, installs that exact bundled helper through a root-owned temporary file into /Library/PrivilegedHelperTools/com.carriez.rustdesk_service as root:wheel mode 0755, byte-compares the deployed helper with the bundled source, then requires the deployed helper directory and executable to be non-symlinks, executable, free of group/world write bits, free of ACL entries, and signed by the same requirement before load. Uninstall unloads launchd and removes both the deployed helper and any install-temp helper. The app bundle is never chowned into a root service path, and no bundle symlink tree is trusted as root code. The dormant update.scpt/update_daemon_agent/.rustdeskupdate-* surface is deleted rather than hardened around, and install no longer accepts an active-user home or copies RustDesk.toml/RustDesk2.toml into root preferences. The macOS privileged service template identity input is constrained: admin-authorized AppleScript/plist rendering no longer reads live CFBundleIdentifier / NSBundle.bundleIdentifier and no longer broad-replaces lowercase rustdesk through root-executed source. Dynamic rendering is limited to validated app-name derived launchd labels, app executable paths, and display/support/log paths; the fixed helper path, helper designated requirement, associated bundle identifier, and runtime app code-signing identifier remain com.carriez.rustdesk / com.carriez.rustdesk_service. Plists are still written with quoted content/path handling and explicit root ownership/mode; daemon log files are recreated under the root-owned log directory; Rust-side helper/reopen launchers use absolute system tool paths rather than caller-controlled PATH; active-console identity is resolved from /dev/console ownership plus the passwd database rather than parsing ls output; and the macOS _service IPC receiver first requires the peer to be the installed app executable under a root-owned, non-symlink, non-group/world-writable bundle path satisfying the pinned Developer ID Team ID plus com.carriez.rustdesk app identifier requirement, then rechecks the deployed helper directory, helper file, ACL state, and the same helper code-signing requirement before admitting the peer. verify.sh and apple-conform-check.sh assert these source invariants.
30Windows installer service-binary root and elevated script authority — the inherited Windows EXE/MSI install surfaces let a user-chosen install directory become the LocalSystem service binary path, while the EXE installer wrote elevated command files under caller-writable staging and MSI kept service/registry shell fallbacks. A local unprivileged or same-user process could turn install/update consent into a persistent SYSTEM service path if the service binary root stayed user-writable or if elevated shell fallbacks consumed mutable caller-controlled paths.FIX R-S11d fixes every EXE service-creation entry point to the Program Files known-folder root, rejects non-default EXE install paths, removes the Flutter path picker, removes MSI public install-folder/browse properties by making the package directory private, resolves elevated EXE cmd.exe through GetSystemDirectoryW, keeps generated EXE command files open read-only with write/delete sharing denied while elevated children consume them, makes EXE sc service-creation failures fatal to the command marker, and deletes MSI service shell fallbacks so service create/start/stop/delete use checked native APIs only. R-S11d-16 separately deletes the persistent installer-side SAS policy write. verify.sh asserts the source shape.
31Windows service-owned IPC process-close actions — the main IPC transport admits same-session same-executable peers, and the inherited Data::Close arm exited the receiver process. In installed Windows service mode a normal same-session RustDesk executable could therefore send Data::Close to a service-owned --server child and drive a service restart/availability transition through transport identity rather than service-owned action authority. The Windows _service receiver also treated Data::Close as a service-loop stop after transport authorization, which made the same service-control action shape dependent on same-session/current-executable identity if a sender path were ever reintroduced.FIX R-S11c closes the control-plane action at the receiver. On the main channel, Data::Close is no longer in the unconditional non-mutating bucket: user-owned receivers still accept user-owned close, but a service-owned receiver accepts main-channel close only from the owning root/LocalSystem service peer; on Windows the loop resolves the named-pipe client token for Data::Close before calling the policy table. On Windows _service, Data::Close now stops the service loop only when the pipe client token is LocalSystem. verify.sh asserts the close authority rule, the Windows token-resolution paths, and absence of an unconditional close classification.
32Windows Amyuni IDD helper launch provenance — the MSI deferred custom action and runtime virtual-display path verified usbmmidd_v2\deviceinstaller64.exe under the installed executable directory, but launched the helper through a bare deviceinstaller64.exe name plus working directory. The runtime path also treated the current executable directory as helper authority unless it was explicitly rebound to the fixed installed service executable and rejected reparse-backed helper/INF objects. In privileged MSI/custom-action and service/runtime contexts, the checked path and executed path must be the same authority-bearing object; the working directory is not executable identity.FIX R-S11d-1 binds the launch to the checked helper path and fixed service root. The MSI action now verifies that usbmmidd_v2 is a directory, verifies that the helper path is a file, and passes the absolute exePath as the CreateProcessW application path. The runtime helper uses handle-level identity to prove the running executable directory is the fixed Program Files service root and the running executable is the fixed installed service executable, derives usbmmidd_v2, deviceinstaller64.exe, and usbmmIdd.inf from that root only, rejects symlink/reparse-backed helper directories, helper files, and INF files, propagates helper-trust failures instead of falling through, and passes paths.exe_path as the CreateProcessW application path. verify.sh asserts the fixed-root file-identity proof, non-reparse helper/INF checks, absolute helper launch in both paths, and rejects the old bare-name, swallowed-error, lossy path/INF, and ShellExecuteA/ShellExecuteW helper-launch shapes.
33Windows Amyuni IDD cleanup completion authority — after helper-path provenance was fixed, MSI uninstall/update still had two ways to hide privileged driver cleanup failure: older builds treated RemoveAmyuniIdd as best-effort helper launch state, and the native SetupAPI path still returned void, skipped hardware-ID/class-installer/remove failures, and let MSI report success without proving the usbmmidd virtual-display device was absent or removed.FIX R-S11d-2 makes both authorities checked. The native SetupAPI path returns HRESULT plus DriverUninstallStatus: complete enumeration proving no present matching hardware ID is a successful no-op, successful removal of all matching present devices is success, and enumeration/property/class-installer/remove failures are fatal unless the AMD64 helper fallback succeeds. The helper fallback runs only after native failure; if native removal failed, missing helper directory/executable is fatal.
